# Machine intelligence in the texts of monetary and financial authorities

Generated from `pack.json` by `node research/ai-money/tools.mjs render`; do not edit by hand. Candidate research pack outside application data. Machine-prepared (Claude family) and audited by the same family; cross-family check by GPT (OpenAI, via Codex) on 2026-11-03, adjudicated on 2026-11-03, with a second pass on two items; maintainer decisions of 2026-11-03 and 2026-11-04 applied, and a bounded follow-up pass of 2026-11-04 (each record or event the adjudication, a decision or the follow-up touched carries a crossFamily mark); not editorially accepted; no human review.

## How to read a record

- A record relates one text to one proposition: it articulates the proposition (a passage meets the criterion), contests it (a passage disputes it or a premise it depends on; the engagement says whether it gives a reason or states the contrary only) or bears on it (it comes close; the missing element is named).
- Each text has a voice. An institutional text is the institution’s. A speech is its speaker’s, with any disclaimer shown; it is not the institution’s position unless the institution says so. A staff paper is its authors’.
- A record says what a text states, on its date. It is not evidence that a risk has materialised, that a forecast is right or that a policy was adopted.
- "Earliest located" means the earliest text found by the recorded search (see the search log), among texts of every voice, never a claim of first statement. The earliest located institutional text is derived from the records and shown beside it.
- An institutional event records what an authority’s own text states. Announced stays announced.

## UA. Machine intelligence and financial stability

### U01. AI raises financial-stability risks through correlated behaviour

**Criterion.** Widespread use of similar AI or machine-learning models, data or techniques by financial firms could make their decisions more alike (herding, uniformity, correlated trading or lending), which could amplify shocks, procyclicality or market volatility.

**Search terms (not names).** herding; herd behaviour; herd behavior; monoculture.

**Earliest located:** Kuroda, 'AI and the Frontiers of Finance' (2017), 2017-04-13 (`am-u01-boj-2017-kuroda-ai`).
**Earliest located institutional text (derived):** FSB, AI and machine learning in financial services (November 2017), 2017-11-01 (`am-u01-fsb-2017-ai-ml`).
**Earliest located contesting text:** Federal Reserve Board, Financial Stability Report, November 2025, Box 1.1, 2025-11 (`am-u01-frb-2025-fsr-nov-contests`).

#### Carney, speech of 25 January 2017 — Bears on (Context)

`am-u01-boe-2017-carney-promise-fintech-context` · 2017-01-25 (delivery) · official’s speech or remarks: Mark Carney (Governor of the Bank of England; Chair of the Financial Stability Board), Bank of England

In a speech of 25 January 2017, Mark Carney, Governor of the Bank of England and Chair of the Financial Stability Board, said that robo-advice and risk management algorithms in wholesale banking and markets may lead to excess volatility or pro-cyclicality through herding, particularly if the algorithms are highly correlated.

_Missing:_ The passage concerns algorithms and robo-advice generally; it does not attribute the herding risk to AI or machine-learning models, data or techniques.

> In wholesale banking and markets, robo-advice and risk management algorithms may lead to excess volatility or increase pro-cyclicality as a result of herding, particularly if the underlying algorithms are overly sensitive to price movements or highly correlated.
>
> — PDF p. 11 (printed p. 11), section on systemic risks of fintech (boe-2017-carney-promise-fintech; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The passage speaks of algorithms in general, within a speech on fintech; it does not name AI or machine learning as the source of the correlation. The speech elsewhere lists “innovations in machine learning” among enabling technologies. A statement of a possible risk, not evidence that it has materialised.

#### Kuroda, 'AI and the Frontiers of Finance' (2017) — Articulates (Earliest located)

`am-u01-boj-2017-kuroda-ai` · 2017-04-13 (delivery) · official’s speech or remarks: Haruhiko Kuroda (Governor of the Bank of Japan), Bank of Japan

In remarks of 13 April 2017, Haruhiko Kuroda, Governor of the Bank of Japan, said that there are concerns that high-frequency and algorithmic trading may increase market volatility, especially if massive trading is based on a similar algorithm, and that applying AI and big data analytics to such trading might intensify these effects.

> At the same time, there are also concerns that HFT and algorithmic trading may increase market volatility, especially if massive trading is based on a similar algorithm
>
> — Section on new issues for market stakeholders and policymakers, paragraph beginning 'High-frequency trading (HFT) and algorithmic trading' (boj-2017-kuroda-ai; primary, supports)

> As such, the application of AI and big data analytics to HFT and algorithmic trading might intensify these negative impacts.
>
> — Same paragraph (boj-2017-kuroda-ai; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ A conditional statement in opening remarks: the concern about similar algorithms is reported as one side of an argument, and the speaker's own claim is that AI 'might intensify' it. Not evidence that such effects occurred; the remarks do not state a Bank of Japan position.

#### FSB, Financial Stability Implications from FinTech (June 2017) — Bears on (Context)

`am-u01-fsb-2017-fintech-fs-context` · 2017-06-27 (publication) · institutional text: Financial Stability Board

The FSB report of 27 June 2017 on financial stability implications from FinTech states that social trading and robo-advice could show greater herding behaviour than traditional portfolio allocation if risk models are highly correlated through reliance on similar algorithms, and notes that it found no empirical evidence so far of convergence of robo-advisors’ algorithms or portfolios.

_Missing:_ The passage does not name AI or machine-learning models; it concerns robo-advice and social trading algorithms generally.

> Social trading and robo-advice could potentially exhibit greater herding behaviour than traditional portfolio allocation methods – for example, if risk models are highly correlated due to reliance on similar algorithms – thereby potentially increasing the amplitude of swings in asset prices.
>
> — Section 3 (financial stability risks), PDF p. 24 (printed p. 20) (fsb-2017-fintech-fs; primary, supports)

> we found no empirical evidence so far on convergence of robo-advisors’ algorithms or portfolios.
>
> — Annex C (robo-advisors), footnote, PDF p. 50 (fsb-2017-fintech-fs; primary, context)

_Qualification:_ The passage concerns robo-advice and social trading algorithms; it does not name AI or machine learning (the report’s glossary says robo-advisors "can also include machine learning"). Recorded as context to the November 2017 articulation.

#### FSB, AI and machine learning in financial services (November 2017) — Articulates (Articulation)

`am-u01-fsb-2017-ai-ml` · 2017-11-01 (publication) · institutional text: Financial Stability Board

The FSB report of 1 November 2017 on artificial intelligence and machine learning in financial services states that if many market participants use similar AI and machine-learning programmes, the resulting correlated risks may entail financial stability risks and, as with any herding behaviour, could amplify financial shocks.

> if many market participants come to use similar AI and machine learning programmes in areas such as credit scoring or financial market activities, the consequent correlated risks may entail financial stability risks.
>
> — Section 4.1, PDF p. 29 (printed p. 25) (fsb-2017-ai-ml; primary, supports)

> While there is no evidence of this occurring to date, this could become relevant with greater adoption of such trading strategies. As with any herding behaviour in the market, this has the potential to amplify financial shocks.
>
> — Section 4.1, PDF p. 29 (printed p. 25) (fsb-2017-ai-ml; primary, supports)

> If a critical segment of financial institutions rely on the same data sources and algorithmic strategies, then under certain market conditions a shock to those data sources … could affect that segment as if it were a single node.
>
> — Section 5.3 "Networks and interconnectedness", PDF p. 35 (printed p. 31) (fsb-2017-ai-ml; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ A statement of what the text says, not evidence that the risk has materialised or that any authority has adopted a policy. The report states that there was no evidence of this occurring at the time and frames it as a possibility with greater adoption; its analysis is described as preliminary.

#### IMF Departmental Paper 2021/024 (Boukherouaa, Shabsigh and others) — Articulates (Articulation)

`am-u01-imf-2021-dp-ai-finance` · 2021-10-22 (publication) · staff paper: El Bachir Boukherouaa, Ghiath Shabsigh, Khaled AlAjmi, Jose Deodoro, Aquiles Farias, Ebru S. Iskender, Alin T. Mirestean, Rangachary Ravikumar, International Monetary Fund

_Disclaimer (PDF p. 3, series note):_ “The views expressed in this paper are those of the author(s) and do not necessarily represent the views of the IMF, its Executive Board, or IMF management.”

In the IMF Departmental Paper of 22 October 2021, IMF staff authors El Bachir Boukherouaa, Khaled AlAjmi, Jose Deodoro, Aquiles Farias and Rangachary Ravikumar write that concentration of third-party AI/ML algorithm providers could drive greater homogeneity in risk assessments and credit decisions, with a risk of uniformity (herding), and that widespread AI/ML use could increase procyclicality.

> The concentration of third-party AI/ML algorithm providers could drive greater homogeneity in risk assessments and credit decisions in the financial sector, which, coupled with rising interconnectedness, could create the conditions for a buildup of systemic risks.
>
> — Chapter 3, section F "Impact on Financial Stability", second bullet (HTML) (imf-2021-dp-ai-finance; primary, supports)

> The widespread use of AI/ML could potentially increase procyclicality of financial conditions.
>
> — Chapter 3, section F, third bullet (HTML) (imf-2021-dp-ai-finance; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ A statement of what the text says, not evidence that the risk has materialised or that any authority has adopted a policy. A staff paper attributed to its authors.

#### PBoC, FinTech Development Plan 2022–2025 (notice of 29 December 2021) — Bears on (Context)

`am-u01-pbc-2021-fintech-plan-2022-2025` · 2021-12-29 (signature) · institutional text: 中国人民银行 (People's Bank of China)

The People's Bank of China's FinTech Development Plan 2022–2025, issued by notice of 29 December 2021, lists the herding effect (羊群效应), together with the algorithmic black box and algorithmic discrimination, among problems that financial institutions are to guard against through stress testing, simulation and parameter tuning.

_Missing:_ The passage names 羊群效应 (herding effect) as an algorithm problem to be prevented but does not state the mechanism (similar models or data making decisions alike) or that it could amplify shocks or volatility.

> 通过临界测试、仿真模拟、参数调优等方式着力防范算法黑盒、羊群效应、算法歧视等问题，提升算法可解释性、透明性、公平性和安全性。
>
> — Attachment, chapter on tasks, item （二十四）筑牢金融与科技风险防火墙 (pbc-2021-fintech-plan-2022-2025; primary, context). English paraphrase: through critical testing, simulation and parameter tuning, guard against problems such as the algorithmic black box, the herding effect and algorithmic discrimination, and raise the explainability, transparency, fairness and security of algorithms.

_Qualification:_ A planning document's task list in Chinese; the English rendering is a paraphrase. It records that the plan names the problem, not that herding has occurred or that measures were implemented.

#### BCBS, newsletter on AI and machine learning (2022) — Bears on (Context)

`am-u01-bcbs-2022-newsletter-aiml` · 2022-03-16 (publication) · institutional text: Basel Committee on Banking Supervision

The Basel Committee's newsletter of 16 March 2022 on AI and machine learning states that its continuing discussions will cover the potential implications of broader usage of AI/ML models for the resilience of individual banks and for financial stability.

_Missing:_ Names financial stability as an area for analysis but states no mechanism of correlated or herding behaviour.

> _Paraphrase:_ The third area named for continuing discussions is the potential implications of broader usage of AI/ML models for the resilience of individual banks and, more broadly, for financial stability.
>
> — Third of the three areas listed for continuing discussions (bcbs-2022-newsletter-aiml; primary, context)

_Qualification:_ The newsletter states it is for information only and is not supervisory guidance.

#### BoE/PRA and FCA, DP5/22 (2022) — Articulates (Articulation)

`am-u01-boe-2022-dp5-22` · 2022-10-11 (publication) · institutional text: Bank of England (including the PRA) and Financial Conduct Authority

The Bank of England (including the PRA) and the Financial Conduct Authority state in Discussion Paper DP5/22 of 11 October 2022 that AI models may become correlated in subtle ways and add to risks of herding or procyclical behaviour in market stress, and that uniformity across firms’ models could amplify procyclical behaviour.

> models may become correlated in subtle ways and add to risks of herding, or procyclical behaviour at times of market stress.
>
> — Executive summary, “Financial markets” (boe-2022-dp5-22; primary, supports)

> algorithms may result in uniformity across models and approaches at multiple firms, which could amplify procyclical behaviour and lead to herding in certain use cases, such as algorithmic trading.
>
> — Chapter 3, para 3.22 (boe-2022-dp5-22; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ A discussion paper inviting responses; it states possible risks and does not record that herding from AI has occurred or adopt a policy.

#### Hernández de Cos, 'Managing AI in banking' (2024) — Bears on (Context)

`am-u01-bis-2024-hdc-managing-ai-banking` · 2024-04-17 (delivery) · official’s speech or remarks: Pablo Hernández de Cos (Chair of the Basel Committee on Banking Supervision and Governor of the Bank of Spain), Bank for International Settlements

_Disclaimer (BIS page, note at the end of the text):_ “The views expressed here are my own and not necessarily those of the BIS or its member institutions.”

In a keynote speech of 17 April 2024, Pablo Hernández de Cos, then Chair of the Basel Committee on Banking Supervision and Governor of the Bank of Spain, said that AI/ML models have yet to be seen across a full financial cycle and could, left unchecked, potentially amplify future banking crises.

_Missing:_ The retrieved passage names no mechanism of similar models, data or techniques making firms' decisions alike; only the opening of the speech was retrieved, and the full text may say more.

> Left unchecked, such models could potentially amplify future banking crises.
>
> — BIS page, paragraph beginning 'My main message is' (bis-2024-hdc-managing-ai-banking; primary, context)

_Qualification:_ The speaker's own views under his disclaimer. Recorded from the opening paragraphs published on the BIS page only.

#### ECB Financial Stability Review, May 2024, special feature on AI — Articulates (Articulation)

`am-u01-ecb-2024-fsr-ai` · 2024-05 (publication) · staff paper: Georg Leitner, Jaspal Singh, Anton van der Kraaij, Balázs Zsámboki, European Central Bank

A special feature of the ECB’s Financial Stability Review of May 2024, prepared by Georg Leitner, Jaspal Singh, Anton van der Kraaij and Balázs Zsámboki, states that widespread AI adoption may harbour the potential for increased herding behaviour and market correlation, and that AI could lead to distorted asset prices, increased correlation, herding or bubbles.

> widespread AI adoption may harbour the potential for increased herding behaviour and market correlation.
>
> — Opening summary paragraph (ecb-2024-fsr-ai; primary, supports)

> As a result, AI may make market participants’ conclusions systematically biased, leading to distorted asset prices, increased correlation, herding behaviour or bubbles.
>
> — Section “Financial stability implications of AI” (ecb-2024-fsr-ai; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ A staff-prepared special feature published in the Financial Stability Review; the page carries no statement that it is the Governing Council’s view. It describes channels and conditions (technological penetration and supplier concentration), not observed herding.

#### BIS, Annual Economic Report 2024, chapter III — Articulates (Articulation)

`am-u01-bis-2024-aer-ch3` · 2024-06-30 (publication) · institutional text: Bank for International Settlements

The BIS Annual Economic Report 2024 (chapter III) states that the behaviour of financial institutions using the same algorithms could amplify procyclicality and market volatility by exacerbating herding, liquidity hoarding, runs and fire sales.

> The behaviour of financial institutions using the same algorithms could amplify procyclicality and market volatility by exacerbating herding, liquidity hoarding, runs and fire sales.
>
> — Section 'Financial system impact of AI', paragraph beginning 'The reliance of market participants on the same handful of algorithms' (bis-2024-aer-ch3; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ It states a possible risk; it does not report observed herding.

#### BIS Working Papers No 1194 (Aldasoro, Gambacorta, Korinek, Shreeti and Stein, 2024) — Articulates (Articulation)

`am-u01-bis-wp-1194` · 2024-06 (publication) · staff paper: Iñaki Aldasoro, Leonardo Gambacorta, Anton Korinek, Vatsala Shreeti, Merlin Stein, Bank for International Settlements

_Disclaimer (PDF p. 2 (inside cover, series note)):_ “The views expressed in them are those of their authors and not necessarily the views of the BIS.”

BIS Working Paper No 1194 of June 2024, by Iñaki Aldasoro, Leonardo Gambacorta, Anton Korinek, Vatsala Shreeti and Merlin Stein, states that machine learning models have compounded the risks of uniformity and model herding, and that correlated autonomous actions by AI agents could produce correlated failures in finance.

> With machine learning models, the risks of uniformity, model herding and network connectedness have only compounded.
>
> — PDF p. 5, introduction (bis-wp-1194; primary, supports)

> Correlated failures, in the form of flash crashes due to correlated autonomous actions might happen in a different form in financial intermediation, asset management, insurance and payments.
>
> — PDF p. 16, section on financial stability (bis-wp-1194; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ A staff working paper, attributed to its authors under the series disclaimer, not a BIS position. It states a risk; it does not report that AI-driven herding has been observed.

#### IMF, GFSR October 2024, chapter 3 — Articulates (Articulation)

`am-u01-imf-2024-gfsr-ch3` · 2024-10-22 (publication) · institutional text: International Monetary Fund

_Disclaimer (eLibrary page header, "Legal-disclaimer"):_ “The views expressed in this publication are those of the IMF staff and do not necessarily represent the views of the IMF’s Executive Directors or their national authorities.”

Chapter 3 of the IMF Global Financial Stability Report of October 2024, prepared by IMF staff of the Monetary and Capital Markets Department, states that AI models may herd and produce similar decisions, especially in stress periods, resulting in procyclical financial stability risks.

> AI models may herd and produce rather similar decisions, especially during stress periods resulting in procyclical financial stability risks.
>
> — Section "Summarizing the Financial Stability Challenges: Current and Prospective", "Increased market speed and volatility under stress" (HTML) (imf-2024-gfsr-ch3; primary, supports)

> The use of AI in capital markets is still relatively nascent, and currently the financial stability risks associated with its adoption appear contained.
>
> — Same section, opening paragraph (HTML) (imf-2024-gfsr-ch3; primary, context)

_Qualification:_ A statement of what the text says, not evidence that the risk has materialised or that any authority has adopted a policy. The chapter also states that AI use in capital markets is still relatively nascent and that its financial stability risks currently appear contained.

#### FSB, The Financial Stability Implications of AI (November 2024) — Articulates (Articulation)

`am-u01-fsb-2024-ai` · 2024-11-14 (publication) · institutional text: Financial Stability Board

The FSB report of 14 November 2024 on the financial stability implications of artificial intelligence states that widespread use of common AI models and data sources could increase correlations in trading, lending and pricing, amplifying market stress, and sets out herding, network externalities, limited choice and lack of transparency as drivers.

> The widespread use of common AI models and data sources could lead to increased correlations in trading, lending, and pricing. This could amplify market stress, exacerbate liquidity crunches, and increase asset price vulnerabilities.
>
> — Executive summary, "Market correlations", PDF p. 5 (printed p. 1) (fsb-2024-ai; primary, supports)

> if FIs primarily use LLMs for internal efficiencies, such as document drafting, employee education and information retrieval, the impact on market correlations is likely to be minimal.
>
> — Section 4.2.2 "Market correlations", PDF p. 25 (printed p. 21) (fsb-2024-ai; primary, context)

_Qualification:_ A statement of what the text says, not evidence that the risk has materialised or that any authority has adopted a policy. The report states that if financial institutions mainly use LLMs for internal efficiencies, the impact on market correlations is likely to be minimal.

#### FSI Insights No 63 (Crisanto, Leuterio, Prenio and Yong, 2024) — Articulates (Articulation)

`am-u01-fsi-2024-insights-63` · 2024-12 (publication) · staff paper: Juan Carlos Crisanto, Cris Benson Leuterio, Jermy Prenio, Jeffery Yong, Financial Stability Institute, Bank for International Settlements

_Disclaimer (PDF p. 2 (inside cover, series note)):_ “The views expressed in them are solely those of the authors and do not necessarily reflect those of the BIS or the Basel-based committees.”

FSI Insights No 63 of December 2024, by Juan Carlos Crisanto, Cris Benson Leuterio, Jermy Prenio and Jeffery Yong, lists herding behaviour among the macroprudential risks of AI, describing it as the amplification of procyclical behaviour through the use of similar data sets and AI models by multiple financial institutions.

> Amplification of procyclical behaviour due to the use of similar data sets and AI models by multiple financial institutions
>
> — PDF p. 13 (printed p. 10), Table 4, row 'Herding behaviour' (fsi-2024-insights-63; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ A staff paper under the FSI series disclaimer. The passage is one entry of a table of risk types; the paper does not report that the risk has materialised.

#### Barr, 'Artificial Intelligence: Hypothetical Scenarios for the Future' (2025) — Articulates (Articulation)

`am-u01-frb-2025-barr-scenarios` · 2025-02-18 (delivery) · official’s speech or remarks: Michael S. Barr (Vice Chair for Supervision (Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System)), Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System

_Disclaimer (Footnote 1):_ “The views expressed here are my own and are not necessarily those of my colleagues on the Federal Reserve Board.”

In a speech of 18 February 2025, Michael S. Barr, then Vice Chair for Supervision of the Federal Reserve Board, said that when generative AI becomes ubiquitous its use could lead to herding behaviour and the concentration of risk, potentially amplifying market volatility.

> When the technology becomes ubiquitous, use of GenAI could lead to herding behavior and the concentration of risk, potentially amplifying market volatility.
>
> — Section 'What Should We Do?', paragraph beginning 'We need to be attuned to the risk in finance' (frb-2025-barr-scenarios; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ Stated as a possibility in the speech's closing section 'What Should We Do?', after two hypothetical scenarios; the speaker's own view with the disclaimer recorded on the document, not a Federal Reserve position, and not evidence that herding has occurred.

#### BoE FPC, Financial Stability in Focus: AI (April 2025) — Articulates (Articulation)

`am-u01-boe-2025-fsif-ai` · 2025-04-09 (publication) · institutional text: Bank of England (Financial Policy Committee)

The Bank of England’s Financial Policy Committee states in its Financial Stability in Focus of 9 April 2025 that the potential for AI-based market participants to take increasingly correlated positions, driven by widespread use of a few models or data sets, is an important consideration from a systemic risk perspective and could amplify shocks in stress.

> From a systemic risk perspective, the potential for AI-based participants to take increasingly correlated positions is an important consideration. This could be driven by the widespread use of a small number of open-source or vendor-provided models or underlying data sets
>
> — Section “Greater use of AI in financial markets” (boe-2025-fsif-ai; primary, supports)

> the potential future use of more advanced AI-based trading strategies could lead to firms taking increasingly correlated positions and acting in a similar way during a stress, thereby amplifying shocks.
>
> — Executive summary (boe-2025-fsif-ai; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The text states that the practical implications are uncertain and that herding risks are not new and are already part of the FPC’s monitoring; it records a judgement of potential risk, not observed AI-driven herding.

#### RBI FREE-AI Committee report (2025) — Articulates (Articulation)

`am-u01-rbi-2025-free-ai-report` · 2025-08-13 (publication) · committee report published by the authority: Pushpak Bhattacharyya (Chairperson), Debjani Ghosh, Balaraman Ravindran, Abhishek Singh, Rahul Matthan, Anjani Rathor, Sree Hari Nagaralu, Suvendu Pati (Member Secretary), Reserve Bank of India (Committee to develop a Framework for Responsible and Ethical Enablement of Artificial Intelligence in the Financial Sector)

The report of the FREE-AI Committee placed on the Reserve Bank of India's website in August 2025 states that when multiple institutions deploy similar AI models or strategies, it could lead to a herding effect in which synchronised behaviours could intensify market volatility and stress.

> When multiple institutions deploy similar AI models or strategies, it could lead to a herding effect where synchronised behaviours could intensify market volatility and stress.
>
> — Chapter 2, section 2.2.8 'Potential Impact on Financial Stability', PDF p. 21 (rbi-2025-free-ai-report; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The committee's text, introduced by reference to the FSB's assessment; the RBI press release does not state that the report is RBI policy. A statement of possible risk, not evidence that herding has been observed.

#### Federal Reserve Board, Financial Stability Report, November 2025, Box 1.1 — Contests (Earliest located)

`am-u01-frb-2025-fsr-nov-contests` · 2025-11 (publication) · institutional text: Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System

The Federal Reserve Board's Financial Stability Report of November 2025 (Box 1.1) states that AI may also help reduce the likelihood of correlated trade execution, by allowing richer information and more complex logic and so a greater diversity of trading signals, and that historical evidence suggests correlated algorithmic trading has not necessarily been detrimental to market quality.

_Engagement:_ engages-premise. The box disputes the premise that AI use makes trading decisions more alike, holding that it may instead diversify trading signals, and questions that correlated trading has been harmful.

> That said, the use of AI may also help reduce the likelihood of correlated trade execution, as it facilitates the use of richer information and more complex logic, potentially leading to a less uniform response to news and to a greater diversity of trading signals among market participants.
>
> — Box 1.1 'Artificial Intelligence and Algorithmic Trading', PDF p. 23 (printed p. 15), paragraph 3 (frb-2025-fsr-nov; primary, supports). Supports the contesting stance.

> historical evidence from algorithmic trading suggests that correlated trading has not necessarily been detrimental to market quality.
>
> — Box 1.1, closing paragraph, PDF p. 24 (printed p. 16) (frb-2025-fsr-nov; primary, supports). Supports the contesting stance.

> While the potential for AI to increase correlated trading and impact market competition cannot be dismissed
>
> — Box 1.1, closing paragraph, PDF p. 24 (printed p. 16) (frb-2025-fsr-nov; primary, context). The box keeps the concern open.

_Qualification:_ The same box also relays the concern ('a long-standing concern', 'cannot be dismissed') and calls for continued monitoring; the contest is partial and balanced within one text. It is the Board's report, not evidence about market outcomes.

In ESRB Advisory Scientific Committee Report No 16 of December 2025, Stephen Cecchetti, Robin L. Lumsdaine, Tuomas Peltonen and Antonio Sánchez Serrano write that widespread use of similar AI models can lead to correlated exposures and amplified market reactions, listing model uniformity among AI features that can exacerbate systemic risk.

>

_Qualification:_ A statement of what the text says, not evidence that the risk has materialised or that any authority has adopted a policy. A footnote states that the executive summary is an edited version of a draft produced by an AI tool.

### U02. Reliance on a few AI providers creates concentration risk

**Criterion.** Dependence of many financial firms on a small number of providers of AI models, data, cloud computing or related services creates concentration or third-party risk for the financial system, such that the failure or disruption of one provider could affect many firms at once.

**Search terms (not names).** concentration risk; third-party dependencies; third-party dependency.

**Earliest located:** FSB, AI and machine learning in financial services (November 2017), 2017-11-01 (`am-u02-fsb-2017-ai-ml`).

### U03. AI increases cyber, fraud and operational risks in finance

**Criterion.** AI, including generative AI, increases the capabilities available for cyberattacks, fraud, impersonation (such as deepfakes) or market manipulation against financial firms, customers or markets, or adds operational risks that financial authorities should take into account.

**Search terms (not names).** deepfake; deepfakes.

> advanced optimisation techniques and predictable patterns in the behaviour of automated trading strategies could be used by insiders or by cybercriminals to manipulate market prices.

#### Brainard, 'What Are We Learning about AI in Financial Services?' (2018) — Articulates (Articulation)

`am-u03-frb-2018-brainard-ai` · 2018-11-13 (delivery) · official’s speech or remarks: Lael Brainard (Governor (Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System)), Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System

_Disclaimer (Footnote 1):_ “These remarks represent my own views, which do not necessarily represent those of the Federal Reserve Board or the Federal Open Market Committee.”

In a speech of 13 November 2018, Lael Brainard, then a member of the Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System, said that AI tools could in future be used to make internet fraud and phishing highly personalized, and that supervised institutions may need their own AI tools to combat outside AI-powered threats.

> in the future, AI tools could be used to make internet fraud and phishing highly personalized.
>
> — Section 'Current Regulatory and Supervisory Approaches', paragraph beginning 'For example, let's take the areas of fraud prevention and cybersecurity' (frb-2018-brainard-ai; primary, supports)

> supervised institutions may need their own AI tools to identify and combat outside AI-powered threats.
>
> — Same paragraph (frb-2018-brainard-ai; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ A forward-looking statement in a speech that carries a 'my own views' disclaimer; it does not report observed AI-enabled fraud and is not a Board position.

#### IMF Departmental Paper 2021/024 (Boukherouaa, Shabsigh and others) — Articulates (Articulation)

`am-u03-imf-2021-dp-ai-finance` · 2021-10-22 (publication) · staff paper: El Bachir Boukherouaa, Ghiath Shabsigh, Khaled AlAjmi, Jose Deodoro, Aquiles Farias, Ebru S. Iskender, Alin T. Mirestean, Rangachary Ravikumar, International Monetary Fund

_Disclaimer (PDF p. 3, series note):_ “The views expressed in this paper are those of the author(s) and do not necessarily represent the views of the IMF, its Executive Board, or IMF management.”

In the IMF Departmental Paper of 22 October 2021, Boukherouaa, AlAjmi, Deodoro, Farias and Ravikumar write that AI/ML adoption increases the scope for cyber threats and brings new cyber risks, and that the cybersecurity regulatory perimeter could be expanded to cover AI/ML-specific threats.

> AI/ML adoption increases the scope for cyber threats and brings in new unique cyber risks.
>
> — Chapter 3, section C "Cybersecurity", opening (HTML) (imf-2021-dp-ai-finance; primary, supports)

> The regulatory perimeter of cybersecurity requirements in the financial sector could be expanded to cover AI/ML-specific cyber threats.
>
> — Chapter 3, section C (HTML) (imf-2021-dp-ai-finance; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ A statement of what the text says, not evidence that the risk has materialised or that any authority has adopted a policy. A staff paper attributed to its authors.

#### BCBS, newsletter on AI and machine learning (2022) — Articulates (Articulation)

`am-u03-bcbs-2022-newsletter-aiml` · 2022-03-16 (publication) · institutional text: Basel Committee on Banking Supervision

The Basel Committee's newsletter of 16 March 2022 on AI and machine learning states that AI/ML deployment, through large data sets, interconnectivity with third parties and cloud use, can create multiple possible points of cyber risk.

> As AI/ML deployment often involves the use of large data sets, interconnectivity with third parties, and the use of cloud technologies, it can also create multiple possible points of cyber risk.
>
> — Second paragraph of the main text, beginning 'In its discussions to date' (bcbs-2022-newsletter-aiml; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The newsletter states it is for information only and is not supervisory guidance; the passage concerns operational and cyber risk in banks, not fraud against customers.

#### IMF FinTech Note 2023/006 (Shabsigh and Boukherouaa) — Articulates (Articulation)

`am-u03-imf-2023-ftn-genai` · 2023-08-22 (publication) · staff paper: Ghiath Shabsigh, El Bachir Boukherouaa, International Monetary Fund

In IMF FinTech Note 2023/006 of 22 August 2023, Ghiath Shabsigh and El Bachir Boukherouaa write that generative AI could be exploited for more sophisticated phishing and impersonation, which could increase identity theft or fraud, and that deepfakes could inflict serious damage on organisations and individuals.

> This emerging technology could be exploited to generate more sophisticated phishing messages and emails or to present opportunities for malicious actors to impersonate individuals or organizations, leading to increased identity theft or fraud.
>
> — Section "Cybersecurity" (HTML) (imf-2023-ftn-genai; primary, supports)

> The proliferation of deepfakes, resulting in more realistic videos, audios, or images, could inflict serious damage on both organizations and individuals.
>
> — Section "Cybersecurity" (HTML) (imf-2023-ftn-genai; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ A statement of what the text says, not evidence that the risk has materialised or that any authority has adopted a policy. A staff note attributed to its authors.

#### BCBS, Digitalisation of finance (2024) — Articulates (Articulation)

`am-u03-bcbs-2024-digitalisation` · 2024-05 (publication) · institutional text: Basel Committee on Banking Supervision

The Basel Committee's report 'Digitalisation of finance' of May 2024 states that fraudsters can use deepfakes, synthetic media generated with AI, to commit account takeovers, loan fraud or wire fraud against bank customers.

> This can include, for example, the use of deepfakes (a type of synthetic media technology that uses AI to manipulate or generate visual and audio content that appears real) to commit account takeovers, loan fraud or wire fraud.
>
> — PDF p. 23 (printed p. 19), section 4, bullet 'Fraud-related risks' (bcbs-2024-digitalisation; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ Describes a fraud risk among the risks of digitalisation; it gives no measure of incidence.

#### ECB Financial Stability Review, May 2024, special feature on AI — Articulates (Articulation)

`am-u03-ecb-2024-fsr-ai` · 2024-05 (publication) · staff paper: Georg Leitner, Jaspal Singh, Anton van der Kraaij, Balázs Zsámboki, European Central Bank

The ECB Financial Stability Review special feature of May 2024, prepared by Georg Leitner, Jaspal Singh, Anton van der Kraaij and Balázs Zsámboki, states that criminals could adapt AI for cyberattacks, market manipulation and deepfakes, and its Box A (prepared by Sándor Gardó and co-authors) states that AI tools will enhance the capabilities of threat actors, including through deepfakes in phishing.

> criminals could fine-tune and spoil otherwise harmless AI for specific operations (e.g. cyberattacks, misinformation, market manipulation, use of deep fakes to undermine confidence in a financial institution, etc.), increasing their threat potential.
>
> — Section “Benefits and risks of AI for financial institutions” (ecb-2024-fsr-ai; primary, supports)

> AI tools will enhance the capabilities of threat actors while also benefiting cybersecurity.
>
> — Box A “The implications of artificial intelligence for cyber risk: a blessing and a curse” (ecb-2024-fsr-ai; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The box also states that AI benefits cyber defence and that it is difficult to assess which side will gain; the text records a change in capabilities, not a measured rise in losses attributable to AI.

#### BIS, Annual Economic Report 2024, chapter III — Articulates (Articulation)

`am-u03-bis-2024-aer-ch3` · 2024-06-30 (publication) · institutional text: Bank for International Settlements

The BIS Annual Economic Report 2024 (chapter III) states that generative AI could vastly expand hackers' ability to write phishing emails and malware, exposing financial institutions and their customers to a greater risk of fraud, and that prompt injection and data or model poisoning add new sources of cyber risk.

> Gen AI could vastly expand hackers' ability to write credible phishing emails or to write malware and use it to steal valuable information or encrypt a company's files for ransom.
>
> — Section 'Financial system impact of AI', paragraph beginning 'The reliance on AI heightens concerns about cyber attacks' (bis-2024-aer-ch3; primary, supports)

> These developments expose financial institutions and their customers to a greater risk of fraud.
>
> — Same paragraph, last sentence (bis-2024-aer-ch3; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ States risks; the same chapter also reports that central bank cyber experts surveyed by the BIS deem gen AI to offer more benefits than risks for cyber security.

#### Cipollone, keynote speech, Rome, 4 July 2024 — Articulates (Articulation)

`am-u03-ecb-2024-cipollone-ai-central-bank` · 2024-07-04 (delivery) · official’s speech or remarks: Piero Cipollone (Member of the Executive Board of the European Central Bank), European Central Bank

In a keynote speech in Rome on 4 July 2024, Piero Cipollone, Member of the Executive Board of the ECB, said that a risk stemming from AI is the degree to which it can be used to spread false information, facilitate fraud or launch cyberattacks, including deepfakes of policymakers in a crisis.

> The second risk stemming from AI is the degree to which it can be used to spread false information and data, facilitate fraud or launch cyberattacks.
>
> — Section “The limits of AI: putting the necessary safeguards in place” (ecb-2024-cipollone-ai-central-bank; primary, supports)

> But it is quite another when a deepfake of a policymaker goes viral – particularly at moments of crisis
>
> — Same section (ecb-2024-cipollone-ai-central-bank; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The speech also states that AI can help detect such attacks; it names a risk for central banks and the public, not a recorded incident. No views disclaimer was found on the page.

#### MAS information paper on GenAI cyber risks (2024) — Articulates (Articulation)

`am-u03-mas-2024-genai-cyber-paper` · 2024-07 (publication) · institutional text: Monetary Authority of Singapore

The Monetary Authority of Singapore's information paper of July 2024 on cyber risks associated with generative AI states that deepfakes can be used by threat actors to enhance the effectiveness of social engineering in cyber attacks and financial scams, and that threat actors have been observed to use generative AI models for more targeted phishing campaigns.

> Deepfakes, which are AI-generated replicas of human video, image, or audio, can be used by threat actors to enhance the effectiveness of their social engineering when carrying out cyber attacks and financial scams.
>
> — Section 2.1 'Deepfakes and GenAI-Enabled Phishing', PDF p. 4 (mas-2024-genai-cyber-paper; primary, supports)

> Threat actors have also been observed to leverage GenAI models to facilitate more targeted phishing campaigns.
>
> — Same section, PDF p. 4 (mas-2024-genai-cyber-paper; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ An information paper to raise financial institutions' awareness; the incidents it cites are reported ('reportedly'), not established by the paper. Not a binding requirement.

#### Räsänen, Euro & talous analysis, 5 September 2024 — Articulates (Articulation)

`am-u03-bof-2024-et-generatiivinen-tekoaly` · 2024-09-05 (publication) · staff paper: Tatu Räsänen, Suomen Pankki (Bank of Finland)

_Disclaimer (End of the article, before the keywords):_ “Tässä artikkelissa esitetyt mielipiteet ovat kirjoittajien omia eivätkä välttämättä edusta Suomen Pankin näkemystä.”

In a Euro & talous analysis of 5 September 2024 published by the Bank of Finland, economist Tatu Räsänen writes (in Finnish) that the spread of generative AI has already increased risks linked to cyber threats, information operations and fraud, that deepfakes make fraud easier, and that generative AI enables more sophisticated cyberattacks.

> Generatiivisen tekoälyn yleistyminen on jo lisännyt kybermaailmaan, informaatiovaikuttamiseen ja huijauksiin liittyviä riskejä.
>
> — Section “Generatiivisen tekoälyn yleistyminen voi lisätä epävakauttaviin kehityskulkuihin liittyviä riskejä rahoitussektorilla”, first paragraph (bof-2024-et-generatiivinen-tekoaly; primary, supports). English paraphrase: the spread of generative AI has already increased risks related to the cyber domain, information operations and scams.

> Huijauksista ja disinformaation levittämisestä on tullut yhä helpompia toteuttaa edistyksellisten syväväärennösten (deepfake) avulla. Tämän ohella generatiivinen tekoäly mahdollistaa sofistikoituneempien kyberhyökkäysten toteuttamisen.
>
> — Same paragraph (bof-2024-et-generatiivinen-tekoaly; primary, supports). English paraphrase: scams and the spreading of disinformation have become ever easier to carry out with advanced deepfakes; in addition, generative AI enables more sophisticated cyberattacks.

_Qualification:_ Quoted in the original Finnish; English paraphrase in the evidence notes. The article cites a US Treasury report for these points and carries a disclaimer that the views are the author’s. It states risk channels, not measured losses.

#### Bailey, Mansion House speech published 14 July 2026 — Articulates (Articulation)

`am-u03-boe-2026-bailey-mansion-house` · 2026-07-14 (publication) · official’s speech or remarks: Andrew Bailey (Governor, Bank of England), Bank of England

In his Mansion House speech published on 14 July 2026, Andrew Bailey, Governor of the Bank of England, said that frontier AI’s ability to find and potentially exploit vulnerabilities in systems represents a major threat to the security of systems and so to financial stability, calling for coordinated testing of frontier models and stronger recovery capabilities.

> Suffice to say, it appears to have moved on very rapidly, and represents a major threat to the security of systems and thus in our world to financial stability.
>
> — Section on frontier AI, paragraph beginning “Let me turn to another aspect of AI” (boe-2026-bailey-mansion-house; primary, supports)

> the ability of cutting-edge models to find, and potentially to exploit, vulnerabilities in systems poses some very important public policy and regulatory challenges
>
> — Following paragraph (boe-2026-bailey-mansion-house; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The speech states the threat and policy responses; it does not report an incident. No views disclaimer was found on the page.

#### Bailey, FSB Chair’s letter to the G20 (28 August 2026) — Articulates (Articulation)

`am-u03-fsb-2026-bailey-g20-letter` · 2026-08-28 (signature) · official’s speech or remarks: Andrew Bailey (Chair, Financial Stability Board (letter headed "The Chair"; the FSB press release of 31 August 2026 names him as Governor of the Bank of England)), Financial Stability Board

In his letter of 28 August 2026 to G20 Finance Ministers and Central Bank Governors, Andrew Bailey, Chair of the Financial Stability Board, wrote that for the financial system the most immediate concern about frontier AI models is their potential impact on cyber risk.

> For the financial system, the most immediate concern is the potential impact of frontier AI on cyber risk. Frontier AI may have the ability materially to alter the speed, scale and economics of cyber risk
>
> — Section "Frontier AI", PDF p. 2 (fsb-2026-bailey-g20-letter; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The Chair’s letter, attributed to him; it is not stated to be an FSB decision.

### U04. The opacity of AI models complicates risk management and supervision

**Criterion.** The limited explainability or interpretability of AI or machine-learning models (the 'black box' problem) makes it harder for financial firms to manage their risks, or for supervisors to assess them or hold firms accountable.

**Search terms (not names).** black box; black-box.

**Earliest located:** FSB, Financial Stability Implications from FinTech (June 2017), 2017-06-27 (`am-u04-fsb-2017-fintech-fs`).

#### FSB, Financial Stability Implications from FinTech (June 2017) — Articulates (Earliest located)

`am-u04-fsb-2017-fintech-fs` · 2017-06-27 (publication) · institutional text: Financial Stability Board

The FSB report of 27 June 2017 on financial stability implications from FinTech states, in its case study on AI and machine learning, that key risks include the opacity and lack of auditability of "black box" algorithms, and that the auditability of AI models used by banks might be difficult to achieve given model validation practices that require models to be explained to regulators.

> Key risks include the opacity and lack of auditability of algorithms (in case of “black boxes”) and undesired changes to market structure.
>
> — Annex G "Case study on AI and machine learning", "Potential to undermine financial stability", PDF p. 60 (printed p. 56) (fsb-2017-fintech-fs; primary, supports)

> Specifically, the “auditability” of the models might be difficult to achieve and not ensured in all cases.
>
> — Same paragraph, PDF p. 60 (printed p. 56) (fsb-2017-fintech-fs; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ A statement of what the text says, not evidence that the risk has materialised or that any authority has adopted a policy. The annex describes its findings as preliminary.

#### FSB, AI and machine learning in financial services (November 2017) — Articulates (Articulation)

`am-u04-fsb-2017-ai-ml` · 2017-11-01 (publication) · institutional text: Financial Stability Board

The FSB report of 1 November 2017 states that the lack of interpretability or auditability of AI and machine-learning methods could become a macro-level risk if not appropriately supervised, and that widespread use of opaque models would make it very difficult for firms and supervisors to predict how model-directed actions affect markets.

> The lack of interpretability or ‘auditability’ of AI and machine learning methods has the potential to contribute to macro-level risk if not appropriately supervised by microprudential supervisors.
>
> — Section 6 "Conclusions", PDF p. 37 (printed p. 33) (fsb-2017-ai-ml; primary, supports)

> it would be very difficult for both firms and supervisors to predict how actions directed by models will affect markets.
>
> — Section 6, PDF p. 38 (printed p. 34) (fsb-2017-ai-ml; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ A statement of what the text says, not evidence that the risk has materialised or that any authority has adopted a policy.

#### Brainard, 'What Are We Learning about AI in Financial Services?' (2018) — Articulates (Articulation)

`am-u04-frb-2018-brainard-ai` · 2018-11-13 (delivery) · official’s speech or remarks: Lael Brainard (Governor (Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System)), Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System

_Disclaimer (Footnote 1):_ “These remarks represent my own views, which do not necessarily represent those of the Federal Reserve Board or the Federal Open Market Committee.”

In a speech of 13 November 2018, Lael Brainard, then a member of the Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System, said that many AI tools and models arrive at conclusions that may be hard to explain and that the challenge of explainability can translate into higher uncertainty about the suitability of an AI approach.

> But AI can introduce additional complexity because many AI tools and models develop analysis, arrive at conclusions, or recommend decisions that may be hard to explain.
>
> — Section 'Current Regulatory and Supervisory Approaches', paragraph beginning 'Let's turn to the related issue of the proverbial "black box"' (frb-2018-brainard-ai; primary, supports)

> The challenge of explainability can translate into a higher level of uncertainty about the suitability of an AI approach, all else equal.
>
> — Next paragraph (frb-2018-brainard-ai; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The speaker's view with a disclaimer; she also states that opaque tools may be used subject to controls. Not a supervisory rule.

#### BIS, Annual Economic Report 2019, chapter III — Bears on (Context)

`am-u04-bis-2019-aer-ch3` · 2019-06-30 (publication) · institutional text: Bank for International Settlements

The BIS Annual Economic Report 2019 (chapter III) states in an endnote that complex and opaque algorithms render biases in machine-learning credit scoring particularly difficult to detect and therefore to prevent.

_Missing:_ Does not say whose task (firms' risk management or supervisors' assessment) the opacity makes harder; the passage concerns detecting discriminatory bias in big tech algorithms.

> The complex and opaque algorithms render biases particularly difficult to detect, and therefore to prevent.
>
> — Endnote 16 (bis-2019-aer-ch3; primary, context)

_Qualification:_ An endnote to a chapter on big tech in finance.

#### BoE and FCA, Machine learning in UK financial services (2019) — Articulates (Articulation)

`am-u04-boe-2019-ml-survey` · 2019-10 (publication) · institutional text: Bank of England and Financial Conduct Authority

The Bank of England and Financial Conduct Authority report “Machine learning in UK financial services” of October 2019 states that machine-learning models are often very large, non-linear and non-parametric, which makes them harder to understand and validate, so that certain forms of risk-taking could go undetected.

> ML models are often very large, non-linear and non-parametric. This makes it harder to comprehensively understand their properties and to validate them. This means certain forms of risk-taking could go undetected.
>
> — PDF p. 14 (printed p. 14), Box 3 “Algorithm complexity, supervision and governance” (boe-2019-ml-survey; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The passage is in a box on supervision and governance in the authorities’ own voice; elsewhere the report relays firms’ views that lack of explainability is a risk. It does not state that undetected risk-taking has occurred.

#### EBA, Report on Big Data and Advanced Analytics (January 2020) — Articulates (Articulation)

`am-u04-eba-2020-bdaa` · 2020-01 (publication) · institutional text: European Banking Authority

The EBA Report on Big Data and Advanced Analytics of January 2020 states that machine-learning models can become "black boxes" whose reasoning is hard to understand and verify, and that lack of explainability could be an important risk for models bought from third parties, which the acquiring institution must be able to validate.

> ML models can quickly become “black boxes”, opaque systems for which the internal behaviour cannot be easily understood, and for which therefore it is not easy to understand (and verify) how a model has reached a certain conclusion or prediction.
>
> — Section 4.2 "Explainability and interpretability", PDF p. 35 (eba-2020-bdaa; primary, supports)

> Lack of explainability could represent an important risk in the case of AI/ML models developed by external third parties and then sold as opaque black box packages.
>
> — Section 4.2, PDF p. 37 (eba-2020-bdaa; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ A statement of what the text says, not evidence that the risk has materialised or that any authority has adopted a policy. The passage concerns explainability as an element of trustworthy use by institutions.

#### Interagency RFI on financial institutions' use of AI (2021) — Articulates (Articulation)

`am-u04-frb-2021-ai-rfi` · 2021-03-31 (publication) · institutional text: Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System, Bureau of Consumer Financial Protection, Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation, National Credit Union Administration, and Office of the Comptroller of the Currency (agencies)

The interagency request for information of 31 March 2021, issued by the Federal Reserve Board, the CFPB, the FDIC, the NCUA and the OCC, states that lack of explainability can inhibit financial institution management's understanding of the conceptual soundness of an AI approach, increasing uncertainty about its reliability and risk when used in new contexts.

> Lack of explainability can also inhibit financial institution management's understanding of the conceptual soundness … of an AI approach, which can increase uncertainty around the AI approach's reliability, and increase risk when used in new contexts.
>
> — Background, 'Explainability', PDF p. 4 (Federal Register vol. 86, p. 16840); the ellipsis omits footnote marker 6 (frb-2021-ai-rfi; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ A background statement in a request for comment by five agencies jointly; it frames questions and sets no requirement.

#### BaFin and Bundesbank, Machine learning in risk models (consultation paper, July 2021) — Articulates (Articulation)

`am-u04-buba-2021-ml-risk-models-consultation` · 2021-07-15 (publication) · institutional text: BaFin and Deutsche Bundesbank

The joint consultation paper of BaFin and the Deutsche Bundesbank of 15 July 2021 states that, as machine-learning models become more complex, their calculations become less transparent for modellers, users, validators and supervisors, making the modelling harder to comprehend and the validity of model output harder to check.

> the details of the calculations are less transparent for modellers, users, validators and supervisors. As a result, it is more difficult to comprehend the modelling and, if applicable, to check the validity of the model output as well.
>
> — PDF p. 13, chapter III.3 ‘Focus on explainability’ (buba-2021-ml-risk-models-consultation; primary, supports)

> The following joint consultation paper of BaFin and Deutsche Bundesbank deals with the concrete use of machine learning (ML) in risk models of Pillars I and II of the regulatory frameworks for banks and insurers.
>
> — Section ‘Consultation 11/2021’ (buba-ml-methods-page; primary, context)

_Qualification:_ The paper also states that the black-box characteristic can be justified by higher predictive ability and that explainable-AI methods are promising; it concerns risk models under Pillars 1 and 2.

_Cross-family check (follow-up 2026-11-04, 2026-11-04 A2.19):_ new record from the follow-up pass (decision 11a, open leads). Second check (2026-11-04): articulation agreed, with the model-performance trade-offs kept.

#### FSI Insights No 35 (Prenio and Yong, 2021) — Articulates (Articulation)

`am-u04-fsi-2021-insights-35` · 2021-08 (publication) · staff paper: Jermy Prenio, Jeffery Yong, Financial Stability Institute, Bank for International Settlements

_Disclaimer (PDF p. 2 (inside cover, series note)):_ “The views expressed in them are solely those of the authors and do not necessarily reflect those of the BIS or the Basel-based committees.”

FSI Insights No 35 of August 2021, by Jermy Prenio and Jeffery Yong, states that humans can be held unambiguously accountable for human-based modelling, and that accountability for AI is complicated by, among other factors, the lack of model explainability.

> Although human-based modelling can suffer from similar weaknesses of AI models – for example bias or errors – a key distinguishing feature of the former is that humans can be held unambiguously accountable.
>
> — PDF p. 4 (printed p. 1), executive summary (fsi-2021-insights-35; primary, supports)

> This challenge is compounded by a number of factors, including (i) the speed and scale of AI adoption by financial institutions; (ii) technical construct of AI algorithms; and (iii) lack of model explainability.
>
> — PDF p. 5 (printed p. 2), executive summary, continuing the same paragraph (fsi-2021-insights-35; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ A staff paper under the FSI series disclaimer; it frames explainability as one of three compounding factors in a challenge of accountability.

#### BCBS, newsletter on AI and machine learning (2022) — Articulates (Articulation)

`am-u04-bcbs-2022-newsletter-aiml` · 2022-03-16 (publication) · institutional text: Basel Committee on Banking Supervision

The Basel Committee's newsletter of 16 March 2022 states that AI/ML models may be more difficult to manage than traditional models because they can be more complex, and names the extent to which model outcomes can be understood and explained as an area for continued discussion.

> In some cases, AI/ML models may be more difficult to manage than traditional models as they can be more complex.
>
> — Second paragraph of the main text, beginning 'In its discussions to date' (bcbs-2022-newsletter-aiml; primary, supports)

> _Paraphrase:_ The first area named for continuing discussions is the extent and degree to which the outcomes of models can be understood and explained.
>
> — First of the three areas listed for continuing discussions (bcbs-2022-newsletter-aiml; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ Informational; not supervisory guidance.

#### BoE/PRA and FCA, DP5/22 (2022) — Articulates (Articulation)

`am-u04-boe-2022-dp5-22` · 2022-10-11 (publication) · institutional text: Bank of England (including the PRA) and Financial Conduct Authority

The Bank of England (including the PRA) and the Financial Conduct Authority state in DP5/22 of 11 October 2022 that the growing complexity of AI models can lead to a lack of explainability or interpretability (the “black box problem”) and that this can lead to prudential risks and may require extra care to ensure accountability and oversight.

> the complexity of AI models is increasing, which can lead to a lack of explainability or interpretability (known as the ‘black box problem’).
>
> — Chapter 3, para 3.18 (boe-2022-dp5-22; primary, supports)

> a lack of explainability or transparency in some AI models may mean extra care or actions are needed to ensure full accountability and sufficient oversight.
>
> — Chapter 2, para 2.17 (boe-2022-dp5-22; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ A discussion paper; it describes risks and does not set new requirements.

#### EBA, Follow-up report on machine learning for IRB models (August 2023) — Articulates (Articulation)

The EBA follow-up report of August 2023 on machine learning for IRB models states that the main challenge discussed for using machine-learning techniques in IRB models is their complexity, which leads to difficulties in explaining their results, traceability and adequate understanding by management functions.

`am-u04-ssm-2026-machado-ai-governance` · 2026-02-24 (delivery) · official’s speech or remarks: Pedro Machado (ECB representative to the Supervisory Board (the page prints “ECB representative to the the Supervisory Board”)), European Central Bank (Banking Supervision)

## UB. Machine intelligence, monetary policy and the economy

### U05. AI can improve nowcasting and policy analysis

**Criterion.** Machine-learning or AI techniques can improve nowcasting, forecasting, the processing of large or unstructured data, or other economic and policy analysis used by central banks.

**Search terms (not names).** nowcasting; nowcast.

**Earliest located:** ECB Working Paper No. 352 (McNelis and McAdam, 2004), 2004-04 (`am-u05-ecb-wp-352`).
**Cross-family check on the headline (follow-up 2026-11-04 (decision 11a, before 2016), 2026-11-04 A1.1, 2026-11-04 A2.14):** Correction: the headline moves from the Bank of England staff working paper of September 2017 to this ECB staff working paper (April 2004), found in the pass before 2016. It also precedes the Czech National Bank paper of 2005 (a lead, decision 16a). Agreed by the second check’s blind search (A1.1). The checker also read the Bank of Canada working paper of January 1999 (a lead, decision 16a) and the Bank of England’s One Bank Research Agenda of February 2015 (C32), which is later than this paper.
**Cross-family check on the headline (2026-11-04 C32):** Second check (C32): the checker read the Bank of England’s One Bank Research Agenda discussion paper (February 2015; launched 25 February 2015) in a March 2015 capture of the official address (web.archive.org; the official addresses now return 404) and finds that its Theme 4 (printed pp. 23–24, PDF pp. 24–25) meets U05 in the Bank’s voice: machine-learning analysis of textual information, including central-bank communications. Agreed. The record is to be drafted from the archived copy before admission part 2; this environment could not reach web.archive.org on 2026-11-04 (connection closed by the proxy). It would replace this report as the earliest located institutional text for U05; it does not displace ECB Working Paper 352 (April 2004).
**Earliest located institutional text (derived):** FSB, AI and machine learning in financial services (November 2017), 2017-11-01 (`am-u05-fsb-2017-ai-ml`).

#### ECB Working Paper No. 352 (McNelis and McAdam, 2004) — Articulates (Earliest located)

`am-u05-ecb-wp-352` · 2004-04 (publication) · staff paper: Paul McNelis, Peter McAdam, European Central Bank

_Disclaimer (PDF p. 3, imprint):_ “The views expressed in this paper do not necessarily reflect those of the European Central Bank.”

ECB Working Paper No. 352 of April 2004, by Paul McNelis and Peter McAdam, reports that “thick” models combining several neural-network models outperform the best-performing linear models in real-time and bootstrap inflation forecasts for euro-area service-price indices, and do as well or sometimes better for consumer and producer price indices across several countries.

> Thick models represent “trimmed mean” forecasts from several neural network models. They outperform the best performing linear models for “real-time” and “bootstrap” forecasts for service indices for the euro area, and do well, sometimes better, for the more general consumer and producer price indices across a variety of countries.
>
> — PDF p. 5, abstract (ecb-wp-352; primary, supports)

> We show in this paper that the linear and neural network specifications converge in their forecasts in such periods.
>
> — PDF p. 6, non-technical summary (ecb-wp-352; primary, context)

_Qualification:_ A staff working paper under the ECB disclaimer; one co-author is outside the ECB. It uses neural networks, a machine-learning technique, without the words “machine learning” or “artificial intelligence”, and reports results for particular indices and periods, not general superiority (the non-technical summary says linear and neural-network forecasts converge in periods without structural change).

_Cross-family check (follow-up 2026-11-04 (decision 11a, before 2016), 2026-11-04 A1.1, 2026-11-04 A2.14):_ new record from the pass before 2016; takes the earliest-located role (correction). Second check (2026-11-04): earliest located text and articulation agreed (blind search A1.1 found the same paper; gains specific to index, country and period).

#### Chakraborty and Joseph, BoE Staff Working Paper No. 674 (2017) — Articulates (Articulation)

`am-u05-boe-2017-swp674-ml-central-banks` · 2017-09 (publication) · staff paper: Chiranjit Chakraborty, Andreas Joseph, Bank of England

_Disclaimer (PDF p. 2 (abstract page), first footnote block):_ “The views expressed in this paper are those of the authors, and not necessarily those of the Bank of England or any of its committees.”

In Bank of England Staff Working Paper No. 674 of September 2017, Chiranjit Chakraborty and Andreas Joseph introduce machine learning for central banking and policy analysis, present a case study projecting UK CPI inflation, and write that machine-learning models generally outperform traditional approaches in prediction tasks.

> We introduce machine learning in the context of central banking and policy analyses.
>
> — PDF p. 2, Abstract (boe-2017-swp674-ml-central-banks; primary, supports)

> Machine learning models generally outperform traditional modelling approaches in prediction tasks, while open research questions remain with regard to their causal inference properties.
>
> — PDF p. 2, Abstract (boe-2017-swp674-ml-central-banks; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ A staff working paper with the authors’ disclaimer; the authors also state that open questions remain about causal inference. Their result is from their own case studies, not an adopted forecasting practice.

_Cross-family check (A1.5, follow-up 2026-11-04):_ earliest-located role moved to ECB Working Paper No. 352, April 2004 (am-u05-ecb-wp-352), found in the pass before 2016; recorded as a correction. The CNB paper of 2005 stays a lead (decision 16a).

`am-u05-ifc-2017-bulletin-44-overview` · 2017-09 (publication) · staff paper: Bruno Tissot, Irving Fisher Committee on Central Bank Statistics, Bank for International Settlements · relay

_Disclaimer (PDF p. 5, note 1 to the overview):_ “The views expressed here are those of the author and do not necessarily reflect those of the Bank for International Settlements (BIS) or the Irving Fisher Committee on Central Bank Statistics (IFC).”

In his overview of IFC Bulletin No 44 (September 2017), Bruno Tissot of the BIS reports a Central Bank of Aruba presentation as finding that machine learning models can incorporate seasonal patterns, non-linearities and lagged effects without ex ante assumptions and appear efficient in predictive capacity for nowcasting tourism data.

> big data algorithms such as machine learning models allow a wide range of effects to be incorporated (for instance, seasonal patterns, non-linearities, lagged effects) without the need to make ex ante assumptions. Third, these new techniques appear efficient in terms of predictive capacity
>
> — PDF p. 11, overview section 3, paragraph beginning 'A presentation by the Central Bank of Aruba' (ifc-2017-bulletin-44; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ A relay: the overview summarises a presentation by Central Bank of Aruba staff; the same passage notes the 'limited interpretability' of 'black box' calculations. Staff voice under the overview's disclaimer.

#### Danmarks Nationalbank, Analysis, 21 January 2019 — Articulates (Articulation)

`am-u05-dn-2019-text-ml-bankruptcy` · 2019-01-21 (publication) · institutional text: Danmarks Nationalbank

Danmarks Nationalbank’s Analysis of 21 January 2019 on text-based machine learning and bankruptcy modelling states, in its summary, that machine-learning methods make data modelling more flexible and allow unstructured data to be used, and that using them to include auditors’ remarks and management reports substantially increases the accuracy of calculated bankruptcy probabilities.

> Benyttes sådanne metoder til at inddrage revisorpåtegninger og ledelsesberetninger fra virksomheders regnskaber til at beregne deres konkurssandsynlighed, øges nøjagtigheden af beregningen væsentligt.
>
> — Landing page, summary paragraph (dn-2019-text-ml-bankruptcy; primary, supports). Translation: if such methods are used to include auditors’ remarks and management reports from firms’ accounts in calculating their bankruptcy probability, the accuracy of the calculation increases substantially.

_Qualification:_ Quoted from the Danish summary on the landing page; the PDF of the Analysis (No. 3, read 2026-11-04, the same bytes as the cross-family checker’s copy) states the same finding in its summary (“Det øger nøjagtigheden af de beregnede konkurssandsynligheder”). The PDF credits Anna Kirstine Hvid and Pia Mølgaard, Quantitative Risk Analysts, Financial Stability (p. 7), and prints no statement that the views are theirs alone: the voice is the Nationalbank’s analytical series, with staff credits, not a claim that the text is anonymous. The modelling serves the Nationalbank’s financial-stability analysis; the underlying working paper of November 2018 is a separate document, not recorded.

_Cross-family check (follow-up 2026-11-04, 2026-11-04 A2.18, 2026-11-04 B25, 2026-11-04 C30):_ new record from the follow-up pass (decision 11a, open leads). Second check (2026-11-04): articulation agreed; the PDF, now read, credits two staff analysts and prints no views disclaimer, so the voice stays institution, qualified.

#### IMF Departmental Paper 2021/024 (Boukherouaa, Shabsigh and others) — Articulates (Articulation)

`am-u05-imf-2021-dp-ai-finance` · 2021-10-22 (publication) · staff paper: El Bachir Boukherouaa, Ghiath Shabsigh, Khaled AlAjmi, Jose Deodoro, Aquiles Farias, Ebru S. Iskender, Alin T. Mirestean, Rangachary Ravikumar, International Monetary Fund

_Disclaimer (PDF p. 3, series note):_ “The views expressed in this paper are those of the author(s) and do not necessarily represent the views of the IMF, its Executive Board, or IMF management.”

In the IMF Departmental Paper of 22 October 2021, Boukherouaa, AlAjmi, Deodoro, Farias and Ravikumar write that AI/ML systems could help central banks improve their understanding of economic and financial developments and support better-tuned monetary and macroprudential policies, and that central bank experiments have focused on near-term forecasting and market sentiment.

> AI/ML systems could help central banks improve their understanding of economic and financial developments, support better-tuned monetary and macroprudential policies, and improve central banks’ operations.
>
> — Chapter 2, section E "Central Banking" (HTML) (imf-2021-dp-ai-finance; primary, supports)

> Experiments and research by central banks have focused on improving the near-term forecasting capacity and the monitoring of market sentiment to inform policy decisions.
>
> — Chapter 2, section E (HTML) (imf-2021-dp-ai-finance; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ A statement of what the text says, not evidence that the risk has materialised or that any authority has adopted a policy. The authors add that the use of AI/ML for policymaking should remain subject to good judgment.

#### Cook, 'Generative AI, Productivity, the Labor Market, and Choice Behavior' (2023) — Bears on (Context)

`am-u05-frb-2023-cook-genai-context` · 2023-09-22 (delivery) · official’s speech or remarks: Lisa D. Cook (Governor (Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System)), Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System

_Disclaimer (Footnote 1):_ “The views I express here are my own and not those of the Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System or the Federal Open Market Committee.”

In a speech of 22 September 2023, Lisa D. Cook, Governor of the Federal Reserve Board, said that in the policy arena an AI black box with no insight into the decision-making process is of limited value, and that she looks on model-generated forecasts with a skeptical eye if they are not coupled with a plausible explanation of their driving factors.

_Missing:_ A statement that AI or machine learning does not, or cannot, improve policy analysis; the passage conditions the value of AI forecasts on an explanation of their drivers.

> In this context, an AI black box with no insight into the decision-making process is of limited value. As a policymaker, I look upon model-generated forecasts with a skeptical eye, if they are not coupled with a plausible explanation for the driving factors behind them.
>
> — Section 'Choice Behavior', paragraph beginning 'Policymakers, too, at all levels of government' (frb-2023-cook-genai; primary, supports). Supports the contesting stance.

_Qualification:_ A conditional reservation, not a denial that AI can improve analysis; in the same speech she states interest in progress on 'explainable AI'. The speaker's view with disclaimer.

`am-u05-bis-2024-aer-ch3` · 2024-06-30 (publication) · institutional text: Bank for International Settlements

> LLMs and gen AI hold promise to overcome both bottlenecks (see Box D).
>
> — Section 'Harnessing AI for policy objectives', paragraph beginning 'These capabilities are particularly promising for nowcasting' (bis-2024-aer-ch3; primary, supports)

> Such sentiment indices can substantially improve forecasts for key macroeconomic quantities, such as unemployment, GDP growth and inflation.
>
> — Box D, paragraph beginning 'Nowcasting models could benefit from deep neural networks' embeddings' (bis-2024-aer-ch3; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ 'Most developed' is an editorial judgement among this group's located texts (a full box on nowcasting). The chapter also states limits on AI in macroprudential use given scarce crisis data.
