### U06. AI's economic effects matter for monetary policy

**Criterion.** The effects of AI on productivity, labour markets, prices, investment or the neutral rate of interest are relevant to the conduct of monetary policy, so that central banks need to assess or respond to them.

**Earliest located:** Haldane, ‘Labour’s Share’, 12 November 2015, 2015-11-12 (`am-u06-boe-2015-haldane-labours-share`).
**Cross-family check on the headline (A1.6, follow-up 2026-11-04, 2026-11-04 A1.2, 2026-11-04 A2.12):** Correction under decision 5a: the headline moves from Carney (14 September 2018) to this speech (12 November 2015), located by the checker (A1.6) and read here on 2026-11-04. Both are speeches.
**Earliest located institutional text (derived):** BIS, Annual Economic Report 2024, chapter III, 2024-06-30 (`am-u06-bis-2024-aer-ch3`).

#### Haldane, ‘Labour’s Share’, 12 November 2015 — Articulates (Earliest located)

`am-u06-boe-2015-haldane-labours-share` · 2015-11-12 (delivery) · official’s speech or remarks: Andrew G Haldane (Chief Economist, Bank of England), Bank of England

_Disclaimer (PDF p. 1, cover page):_ “The views are not necessarily those of the Bank of England or the Monetary Policy Committee.”

In a speech of 12 November 2015 to the Trades Union Congress, Andrew G Haldane, Chief Economist of the Bank of England, discussed smart machines and artificial intelligence as substitutes for human work, asked what these labour-market trends imply “for the economy and monetary policy”, and set out how a flat rather than rising labour share would lower the MPC’s projected paths for wages and inflation.

> One source of such advantage is that Artificial Intelligence (AI) – the machine brain - differs in several important respects from human intelligence – the human brain.
>
> — PDF p. 13 (printed p. 13) (boe-2015-haldane-labours-share; primary, supports)

> Turning to the nearer-term, what if any implications do these labour market trends carry for the economy and monetary policy?
>
> — PDF p. 16 (printed p. 16) (boe-2015-haldane-labours-share; primary, supports)

> That might arise because technology has made it easier and cheaper than ever before to substitute labour for capital, man for machine. That is the very essence of the Second Machine Age hypothesis.
>
> — PDF p. 17 (printed p. 17) (boe-2015-haldane-labours-share; primary, supports)

> So how different might the MPC’s projections look if the labour share was, say, flat rather than rising over the next few years?
>
> — PDF p. 17 (printed p. 17) (boe-2015-haldane-labours-share; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ AI is one part of a wider technological change (“smart machines”, the “Second Machine Age”), as in Carney 2018; the monetary-policy passage concerns the substitution of capital for labour in general and a projection scenario, not AI alone or a policy decision. The speaker’s view, with disclaimer.

_Cross-family check (A1.6, follow-up 2026-11-04, 2026-11-04 A1.2, 2026-11-04 A2.12):_ drafted under decisions 5a and 11a; earliest-located role moved here from Carney 2018 (correction). Second check (2026-11-04): earliest located text and qualified whole-text articulation agreed (blind search A1.2 found the same speech; a scenario, not a causal finding).

#### Carney, Whitaker Lecture, 14 September 2018 — Articulates (Articulation)

`am-u06-boe-2018-carney-future-of-work` · 2018-09-14 (delivery) · official’s speech or remarks: Mark Carney (Governor of the Bank of England), Bank of England

In the Whitaker Lecture of 14 September 2018, Mark Carney, Governor of the Bank of England, said that “the appropriate setting of monetary policy will be influenced by” the structural changes of a Fourth Industrial Revolution, which he described as driven by advances in AI, automation and interconnectedness, including through the labour market, price dynamics and the equilibrium rate of interest.

> We are on the cusp of a Fourth Industrial Revolution, which has the potential to transform fundamentally the nature of both work and commerce through advances in AI, automation and interconnectedness.
>
> — PDF p. 3 (printed p. 3) (boe-2018-carney-future-of-work; primary, supports)

> The appropriate setting of monetary policy will be influenced by the structural changes wrought by the Fourth Industrial Revolution, including in the labour market, by its impact on price dynamics and on the equilibrium rate of interest.
>
> — PDF p. 10, section “Monetary Policy’s Role in Managing the Transition” (boe-2018-carney-future-of-work; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The speech speaks of a “Fourth Industrial Revolution” of which AI is one named component, not of AI alone. It states an expected influence on policy, not a policy decision.

_Cross-family check (A1.6, follow-up 2026-11-04):_ earliest-located role moved to Haldane, 12 November 2015 (am-u06-boe-2015-haldane-labours-share), under decision 5a; recorded as a correction. This record is unchanged otherwise.

#### Cook, 'Generative AI, Productivity, the Labor Market, and Choice Behavior' (2023) — Articulates (Articulation)

`am-u06-frb-2023-cook-genai` · 2023-09-22 (delivery) · official’s speech or remarks: Lisa D. Cook (Governor (Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System)), Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System

_Disclaimer (Footnote 1):_ “The views I express here are my own and not those of the Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System or the Federal Open Market Committee.”

In a speech of 22 September 2023, Lisa D. Cook, Governor of the Federal Reserve Board, said that she focuses on AI as a monetary policymaker because of the Federal Reserve's dual mandate, and that the impact of AI on the economy and monetary policy will depend on whether it is a general purpose technology.

> Why do I focus on AI as a monetary policymaker? The Federal Reserve's dual mandate is to promote maximum employment and stable prices.
>
> — Introduction, paragraph beginning 'Why do I focus on AI as a monetary policymaker?' (frb-2023-cook-genai; primary, supports)

> The impact of AI on the economy and monetary policy will depend on whether AI is just another app or something more profound.
>
> — Section 'AI and Productivity', first paragraph (frb-2023-cook-genai; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The speaker's view with disclaimer; it links AI's productivity and labour-market effects to the dual mandate but proposes no policy response. Bowman (22 November 2024) and later speeches restate the link.

>

_Qualification:_ States relevance for central bank mandates; the chapter's section on macroeconomic effects presents scenarios in which AI's initial effect on inflation could be disinflationary or inflationary.

In a keynote speech in Rome on 4 July 2024, Piero Cipollone, Member of the Executive Board of the ECB, said that AI could affect the economy in areas particularly relevant for monetary policy, including prices, monetary policy transmission and the natural rate of interest, which could move up or down depending on AI’s effects.

>

>
> — Section “The natural rate of interest” (ecb-2024-cipollone-ai-central-bank; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ States channels and directions of possible effects; the speaker says the ECB will monitor them. Not a monetary policy decision.

#### Barr, 'Artificial Intelligence and the Labor Market' (2025) — Articulates (Articulation)

In a speech of 9 May 2025, Michael S. Barr, Governor of the Federal Reserve Board, said that AI may require monetary policymakers to reassess their estimates of the natural rate of unemployment (u*) and that a permanently higher productivity growth rate of the kind that might arise from AI tends to raise the neutral interest rate (r*).

> First, AI may require monetary policymakers to reassess our estimates of the natural rate of unemployment, which informs our assessment of the cyclical state of the economy and thus the appropriate stance of monetary policy.
>
> — Section 'Looking Ahead', paragraph beginning 'First, AI may require monetary policymakers' (frb-2025-barr-labor-scenarios; primary, supports)

> Economic theory suggests that a permanently higher growth rate of productivity, of the kind that might arise under either AI scenario, tends to raise r*.
>
> — Section 'Looking Ahead', paragraph beginning 'Another related consideration relevant for monetary policy' (frb-2025-barr-labor-scenarios; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ Most developed among this group's texts in naming two channels (u* and r*) and how they would be monitored; conditional on scenarios, the speaker's view with disclaimer, not an FOMC estimate.

_Qualification:_ The passage speaks of technological innovation in general, with agentic AI named; it is a general statement of relevance, not an assessment of the size of AI’s effects.

>

#### Bech-Moen (Norges Bank), speech, 29 January 2026 — Articulates (Articulation)

`am-u06-nb-2026-bech-moen-speech` · 2026-01-29 (delivery) · official’s speech or remarks: Ole Christian Bech-Moen (Executive Director of Monetary Policy, Norges Bank), Norges Bank

In a speech of 29 January 2026, Ole Christian Bech-Moen, Executive Director of Monetary Policy at Norges Bank, said that Norges Bank is assessing how AI will affect the neutral rate and therefore its assessment of interest-rate levels, and that to the extent AI boosts productivity AI could contribute to an increase in the neutral rate.

> And so here we are today, in real time, wondering how AI will affect the neutral rate and therefore our assessment of interest rate levels in the slightly longer term.
>
> — PDF p. 8, slide ‘“Real-time” estimates and model estimates of the neutral real rate’ (nb-2026-bech-moen-speech; primary, supports)

> To the extent that AI boosts productivity ahead or economic agents believe strongly enough that this will happen, AI could contribute to an increase in the neutral rate.
>
> — PDF p. 8, same passage (nb-2026-bech-moen-speech; primary, supports)

> This translation was generated by ChatGPT and post-edited by Norges Bank.
>
> — PDF p. 1, heading (nb-2026-bech-moen-speech; primary, context)

> I den grad KI løfter produktiviteten fremover – eller at aktørene i økonomien tror sterkt nok på at det vil skje – kan KI bidra til en oppgang i den nøytrale renten.
>
> — PDF p. 8, plansje on the neutral real rate (Norwegian speaking notes) (nb-2026-bech-moen-notes-no; primary, supports). The original Norwegian of the second English quotation.

_Qualification:_ The Norwegian speaking notes (“Talepunkter til presentasjonen”, published with the Norwegian page on 29 January 2026 at 16:00) are the original published expression; the English text, on the English page published on 2 February 2026, is a machine translation post-edited by Norges Bank, as it states, so its wording is not the delivered wording. Both are published notes, not a transcript of the delivered speech. The speech illustrates possibilities with model simulations and states no policy decision. No disclaimer is printed.

_Cross-family check (follow-up 2026-11-04, 2026-11-04 A2.10, 2026-11-04 B26):_ new record from the follow-up pass (decision 11a, open leads). Second check (2026-11-04): articulation and delivery date agreed; the Norwegian speaking notes, located by the checker, are recorded as the original published expression and the English text as Norges Bank’s post-edited machine translation.

_Qualification:_ The speaker states that the views are his own and not the Governing Council’s. He also states that the latest staff estimates of R* show no material change; the speech does not announce a policy response.

#### Bunge, speech, Stockholm, 19 May 2026 — Articulates (Articulation)

`am-u06-rb-2026-bunge-speech` · 2026-05-19 (delivery) · official’s speech or remarks: Aino Bunge (First Deputy Governor (as given on the Riksbank news page for the speech; the speech PDF names the speaker without an office)), Sveriges Riksbank

In a speech of 19 May 2026, Aino Bunge, First Deputy Governor of Sveriges Riksbank, said that AI can drive policy rates up or down through productivity, the neutral rate, investment demand and asset prices, and that while AI has not yet affected current monetary policy, the Riksbank will have to monitor developments.

>
> — Riksbank news page on the speech, second paragraph (rb-2026-bunge-ai-monetary-policy; primary, context). Gives the speaker’s office, which the speech PDF does not print.

_Qualification:_ The speaker states that AI has not yet penetrated the labour market and productivity enough to affect current monetary policy; the record captures the statement that AI’s effects are relevant to policy and must be monitored, not a claim that they are already shaping it.

#### BIS, Annual Economic Report 2026, chapter I — Articulates (Articulation)

`am-u06-bis-2026-aer-ch1` · 2026-06 (publication) · institutional text: Bank for International Settlements

In a speech of 10 September 2026, Pablo Hernández de Cos, General Manager of the BIS, said that AI is making the economy harder to read, increasing uncertainty around the transmission of monetary policy, and can widen errors in estimating potential output and the natural rate of interest.

#### Pan Gongsheng, keynote at the 2026 Financial Street Forum — Articulates (Articulation)

`am-u06-pbc-2026-pan-jrjlt` · 2026-10-19 (posting) · official’s speech or remarks: Pan Gongsheng (潘功胜) (中国人民银行行长 (Governor of the People's Bank of China)), 中国人民银行 (People's Bank of China)

In a keynote posted by the People's Bank of China on 19 October 2026, Governor Pan Gongsheng said that, in the discussions of international organisations and central banks, issues worth attention include that AI's demand and supply effects may differ in timing, raising inflation in the short term and lowering it through productivity in the medium to long term, and that the PBoC will prudently assess the impact of AI on the macroeconomy, financial markets and monetary policy.

> 一是人工智能产生的需求和供给效应可能存在时差，短期会改变微观效率、拉动投资、推升通胀，中长期可以提升整体生产率、降低通胀水平。
>
> — 第四个问题，关于人工智能影响和金融政策响应, point 一 (pbc-2026-pan-gongsheng-jrjlt; primary, supports). English paraphrase: first, AI's demand and supply effects may come at different times; in the short term they change micro-level efficiency, drive investment and push up inflation; in the medium to long term they can raise overall productivity and lower inflation.

> 审慎评估人工智能技术演进对宏观经济、金融市场、货币政策等的影响，前瞻性做好政策考量和准备。
>
> — Same section, PBoC work item 一 (pbc-2026-pan-gongsheng-jrjlt; primary, supports). English paraphrase: prudently assess the impact of the evolution of AI technology on the macroeconomy, financial markets, monetary policy and so on, and make policy considerations and preparations in advance.

_Qualification:_ The speaker presents the timing point as an issue raised in discussions of international organisations and central banks ('从目前国际组织、各国中央银行讨论来看'); the commitment to assess is announced work, not a completed assessment. English renderings are paraphrases.

## UC. Machine intelligence in payments and money

### U07. AI agents will change payment flows

**Criterion.** AI agents acting for people or firms will or could initiate, negotiate or execute payments and transactions, changing how payments are made or the demands placed on payment systems.

**Usual names.** agentic payments; agentic commerce.

**Earliest located:** BIS Working Papers No 1310 (Aldasoro and Desai, 2025), 2025-11-10 (`am-u07-bis-wp-1310`).
**Cross-family check on the headline (A1.7, A2.42, 2026-11-04 A1.3, 2026-11-04 A2.9):** Under decision 15a Waller (29 September 2025) is context, and under decision 6a the ECB innovation platform outcome report (September 2025), which relays workshop participants’ ideas, cannot hold the headline. The headline moves from Waller to this staff working paper (10 November 2025). It was an articulation in the checked pack and was not itself a blind item; the short check of 2026-11-04 covers the role. The second check (A1.3) proposes BIS Working Paper 1194 (June 2024) instead, on a whole-document reading; held for the maintainer under the override rule, both readings under am-u07-bis-wp-1194.
**Earliest located institutional text (derived):** MAS media release on SAFR (2026), 2026-07-03 (`am-u07-mas-2026-safr-release`).

#### BIS Working Papers No 1194 (Aldasoro, Gambacorta, Korinek, Shreeti and Stein, 2024) — Bears on (Context)

`am-u07-bis-wp-1194` · 2024-06 (publication) · staff paper: Iñaki Aldasoro, Leonardo Gambacorta, Anton Korinek, Vatsala Shreeti, Merlin Stein, Bank for International Settlements

_Disclaimer (PDF p. 2 (inside cover, series note)):_ “The views expressed in them are those of their authors and not necessarily the views of the BIS.”

BIS Working Paper No 1194 of June 2024, by Aldasoro, Gambacorta, Korinek, Shreeti and Stein, lists, in its table of the impact of successive AI technologies, faster payment flows and fraud prevention as opportunities of AI agents in payments.

_Missing:_ A table entry only; does not state that AI agents would initiate, negotiate or execute payments for people or firms.

> Faster payment flows, fraud prevention
>
> — PDF p. 14, Table 1, row 'Payments', column 'AI Agents', opportunities (bis-wp-1194; primary, context)

_Qualification:_ A staff working paper under the series disclaimer.

_Cross-family check (2026-11-04 A1.3):_ held for the maintainer under the override rule (earliest-located role). The checker reads this paper as U07’s earliest located text; the preparer reads bears on. The record stays context and BIS Working Paper 1310 keeps the role until the maintainer decides.

_Held for the maintainer._ Checker: articulates, qualified, at whole-document level: Table 1 (PDF p. 14) puts “faster payment flows” under the opportunities of AI agents in payments, and the text (PDF pp. 15–16) says that AI agents “could soon autonomously design, market, and sell financial products and services”; together these meet the transactions limb. The checker adds that the table alone is insufficient and that, if the criterion requires an explicit statement that agents initiate payments, the paper bears on U07 and the role should be held. Preparer: bears on: the selling sentence concerns AI agents in finance in general (it extends autonomous action “from trading to other parts of finance”), not payments, and states no change to how payments are made or to the demands on payment systems; the payments link is a table cell without explanation. WP 1310 (November 2025) states agent-run payment management directly.

#### Waller, 'The Next Frontier of Payments Innovation' (Sibos 2025) — Bears on (Context)

`am-u07-frb-2025-waller-sibos-context` · 2025-09-29 (delivery) · official’s speech or remarks: Christopher J. Waller (Governor (Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System)), Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System

_Disclaimer (Footnote 1):_ “The views expressed here are my own and not necessarily those of my colleagues on the Federal Reserve Board or the Federal Open Market Committee.”

In a speech of 29 September 2025, Christopher J. Waller, Governor of the Federal Reserve Board, said that AI can further improve the efficiency of payments, including through agentic AI executing tasks on behalf of a person or company quickly and cheaply through AI agents.

_Missing:_ The passage names agentic AI "executing tasks on behalf of a person or company" as one way AI can improve the efficiency of payments; it does not state that AI agents initiate, negotiate or execute payments or transactions, and the next sentence concerns compliance.

> AI can further improve the efficiency of payments in a number of ways, including through automating manual tasks, detecting fraud or compliance risks, and now with agentic AI, executing tasks on behalf of a person or company quickly and cheaply through the use of AI agents.
>
> — Section 'Assessing benefits of the next frontier of innovations', paragraph beginning 'Let's turn from DLT to AI' (frb-2025-waller-sibos; primary, context)

_Qualification:_ A brief statement within a speech mainly on distributed ledgers and stablecoins; it does not describe how payment systems would change. The speaker's view with disclaimer. His speech of 20 August 2025 called agentic AI 'the next wave' for payments innovators without stating that agents make payments (see searchLog).

_Cross-family check (A1.7, A2.42, second pass 2026-11-03):_ decided: bears on (context), the checker’s reading, which the second pass held; settled under decision 15a. Former id am-u07-frb-2025-waller-sibos.

The ECB’s digital euro innovation platform outcome report of September 2025 relays ideas from workshop participants that AI-powered digital euro wallets could automatically handle payments such as subscriptions or savings without users approving every transaction.

> Another topic that was discussed during several workshops was the use of artificial intelligence in the digital euro ecosystem. The following examples illustrate participants’ ideas on what this could look like.
>
> — PDF p. 17 (printed p. 16), section 3.2.4 “Artificial intelligence and the digital euro” (margin caption “AI-powered wallets for smarter, more efficient transactions”) (ecb-2025-de-ip-outcome-report; primary, supports)

> For example, they could automatically handle payments such as streaming subscriptions or monthly savings, without users having to approve every single transaction.
>
> — Same section (ecb-2025-de-ip-outcome-report; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ A relay: the report presents participants’ ideas, not the ECB’s assessment or plans, and the digital euro has not been issued. The passage speaks of “smart wallets” under a heading on AI rather than of “AI agents”.

_Cross-family check (B67, A1.7):_ corrected: section heading in the locator. Relay classification agreed. Under decision 6a a relay cannot hold an earliest-located role.

#### Välimäki, opening remarks, Helsinki, 5 November 2025 — Bears on (Context)

In opening remarks on 5 November 2025, Tuomas Välimäki, Member of the Board of the Bank of Finland, asked how soon customers might rely on personal AI agents to manage their finances autonomously instead of making their own spending or investment decisions.

#### BIS Working Papers No 1310 (Aldasoro and Desai, 2025) — Articulates (Earliest located)

`am-u07-bis-wp-1310` · 2025-11-10 (publication) · staff paper: Iñaki Aldasoro, Ajit Desai, Bank for International Settlements

_Disclaimer (PDF p. 3, note to the title):_ “The views expressed in this paper are solely those of the authors and do not necessarily reflect those of the Bank for International Settlements (BIS) or the Bank of Canada (BoC).”

BIS Working Paper No 1310 of November 2025, by Iñaki Aldasoro and Ajit Desai, reports experiments in which a generative AI agent performed intraday liquidity management in a simulated wholesale payment system, and writes that routine cash-management tasks could be automated using general-purpose large language models.

> we evaluate whether a generative artificial intelligence (AI) agent can perform high-level intraday liquidity management in a wholesale payment system.
>
> — PDF p. 3, abstract (bis-wp-1310; primary, supports)

> These findings suggest that routine cash-management tasks could be automated using general-purpose large language models, potentially reducing operational costs and improving intraday liquidity efficiency.
>
> — PDF p. 3, abstract (bis-wp-1310; primary, supports). The PDF text layer breaks 'cash-management' across a line.

_Qualification:_ A staff working paper under the series disclaimer, based on prompt-based simulations; the agent issued recommendations and deferred to human oversight in anomalous scenarios. It does not show agents executing live payments, and concerns banks' payment operations, not consumer commerce.

_Cross-family check (A1.7, A2.42, 2026-11-04 A1.3, 2026-11-04 A2.9):_ earliest-located role assigned under decisions 15a and 6a. Second check (2026-11-04): articulation, staff disclaimer and simulation limits agreed (A2.9); the date note now separates the title-page date from the BIS release date. The role is held for the maintainer on A1.3 (see am-u07-bis-wp-1194).

#### Breeden, speech published 30 June 2026 — Articulates (Articulation, Earliest located use of the name)

`am-u07-boe-2026-breeden-agents-of-change` · 2026-06-30 (publication) · official’s speech or remarks: Sarah Breeden (Deputy Governor, Financial Stability), Bank of England

In a speech published on 30 June 2026, Sarah Breeden, Deputy Governor for Financial Stability at the Bank of England, said that firms are working to let AI agents execute transactions rather than only recommend them, that the Bank is designing retail payments infrastructure to support agentic payment journeys, and that the biggest issues are likely to be for regulation and industry standards, naming consent, liability and the fragmentation of protocols.

> consumers and merchants have so far mainly used AI agents in recommendation mode, with humans still executing transactions. But as agentic technology advances, tech firms, payment systems and merchants are working to automate that final step
>
> — Section “Agentic payments and commerce” (boe-2026-breeden-agents-of-change; primary, supports)

> the Bank is leading a public-private partnership to design the next generation of retail payments infrastructure, ensuring it can support agentic payment journeys
>
> — Same section (boe-2026-breeden-agents-of-change; primary, supports)

> What these two examples – agentic commerce and agentic trading – both highlight is that, as AI capabilities increase, we must keep asking whether existing, technology-agnostic regulatory frameworks remain sufficient.
>
> — Section 'Agentic payments and commerce', closing paragraph (boe-2026-breeden-agents-of-change; primary, supports). Added at the audit (2026-11-02) as the passage that uses the usual name.

_Qualification:_ States that consumers and merchants have so far mainly used agents in recommendation mode; the change is described as in progress, not complete. Attributed to the speaker; no views disclaimer was found on the page.

#### Bailey, Mansion House speech published 14 July 2026 — Articulates (Articulation)

`am-u07-boe-2026-bailey-mansion-house` · 2026-07-14 (publication) · official’s speech or remarks: Andrew Bailey (Governor, Bank of England), Bank of England

>
> — Same section (boe-2026-bailey-mansion-house; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ Framed as questions of law and governance for public policy; the speaker says these are not questions for the Bank to answer. No views disclaimer was found on the page.

#### Waller, 'Payments in the Age of AI Agents' (Sibos 2026) — Articulates (Articulation)

`am-u07-frb-2026-waller-agents` · 2026-09-29 (delivery) · official’s speech or remarks: Christopher J. Waller (Governor (Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System)), Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System

_Disclaimer (Footnote 1):_ “The views expressed here are my own and not necessarily those of my colleagues on the Federal Reserve Board or the Federal Open Market Committee.”

In a speech of 29 September 2026, Christopher J. Waller, Governor of the Federal Reserve Board, described an agent-delegated model of agentic commerce in which a buyer grants an AI agent authority to shop and make payments on the buyer's behalf, and said that agentic transactions could materially change the frequency and timing of payments.

> In the agent-delegated model, a buyer grants authority to an AI agent to shop and make payments on their behalf.
>
> — Section 'Developments in Agentic Commerce', second paragraph (frb-2026-waller-agents; primary, supports)

> Agentic transactions could materially change the frequency and timing of payments, as well as other characteristics.
>
> — Section 'Looking Ahead' (frb-2026-waller-agents; primary, supports)

> The question shifts from proving that a buyer is an authorized payer to proving that an agent has the authority to pay on the buyer's behalf.
>
> — Section 'Developments in Agentic Commerce', paragraph beginning 'The biggest barrier to scaling agentic commerce' (frb-2026-waller-agents; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ Most developed among this group's texts: it distinguishes agent-assisted and agent-delegated models and discusses authentication, liability, fraud, micropayments and standards. It reports market participants' views from a Federal Reserve roundtable of 26 May 2026 alongside the speaker's own; the speaker's view with disclaimer, not a Board position.

### U08. AI agents' transactions bear on tokenised money and stablecoins

**Criterion.** Transactions initiated by AI agents would use, require or favour programmable or tokenised forms of money, stablecoins or tokenised deposits, or AI would interact with such forms of money in ways that matter for the monetary system.

**Search terms (not names).** programmable money.

**Earliest located:** no located text meets the criterion.
**Cross-family check on the headline (A1.8, second pass 2026-11-03):** Decision 7a: no located text meets the criterion. The checker’s narrower reading is noted: in its second pass it reads this passage as stating U08’s second part, qualified that it concerns compliance and states no need for stablecoins, which would make it the earliest located text.

#### BIS, Annual Economic Report 2025, chapter III — Bears on (Context)

`am-u08-bis-2025-aer-ch3` · 2025-06-29 (publication) · institutional text: Bank for International Settlements

The BIS Annual Economic Report 2025 (chapter III) states that, in next-generation correspondent banking on a tokenised platform, further efficiency can be achieved by embedding pre-screening checks into the payment instruction and compliance reporting capabilities through AI agents.

_Missing:_ AI agents are placed in compliance on a tokenised platform; the passage does not state that agents' transactions use, require or favour tokenised money or stablecoins (first limb). On the second limb, the agents act in compliance reporting (Box B describes them as copilots for suspicious-activity reports), not in a way the text says matters for the monetary system.

> In next-generation correspondent banking, further efficiency can be achieved by embedding pre-screening checks directly into the payment instruction and compliance reporting capabilities through AI agents (Box B).
>
> — Section 'The promise of tokenisation', paragraph beginning 'In strengthening AML/KYC compliance' (bis-2025-aer-ch3; primary, context)

_Qualification:_ The chapter's subject is the tokenised unified ledger; AI agents appear in a compliance role (Box B).

_Cross-family check (A1.8, second pass 2026-11-03):_ decided: bears on (context), the preparer’s reading (decision 7a, 2026-11-04). U08 shows “no located text meets the criterion”.

_Readings (maintainer decision 7a (preparer’s reading))._ Checker: second pass (option b): AI agents embedded in payment instructions and compliance reporting on a tokenised correspondent-banking platform, in a chapter on the next-generation monetary system, state the second part of the criterion, with a qualification; no need for stablecoins is stated. Preparer: bears on: the agents do compliance work on a tokenised platform; the passage does not say that this interaction matters for the monetary system.

## UD. Machine intelligence in supervision, risk management and central banks' own work

### U09. Supervisors should use AI

**Criterion.** Financial supervisors or regulators can, or should, use AI or machine-learning tools (supervisory technology, 'suptech') to carry out supervision, including detecting misconduct, money laundering or emerging risks.

**Usual names.** suptech.

**Earliest located:** BCBS Working Paper No. 4 (Sahajwala and Van den Bergh, 2000), 2000-12 (`am-u09-bcbs-2000-wp4-early-warning`).
**Cross-family check on the headline (follow-up 2026-11-04 (decision 11a, before 2016), 2026-11-04 A1.4, 2026-11-04 A2.13, 2026-11-04 B27):** Correction: the headline moves from the FSB report of 27 June 2017 to this BCBS staff working paper (December 2000), found in the pass before 2016. The FSB report stays the earliest located institutional text and the earliest located use of “SupTech”. It also precedes Bauguess (SEC, 2016; a lead, decision 10a).
**Earliest located institutional text (derived):** FSB, Financial Stability Implications from FinTech (June 2017), 2017-06-27 (`am-u09-fsb-2017-fintech-fs`).

#### BCBS Working Paper No. 4 (Sahajwala and Van den Bergh, 2000) — Articulates (Earliest located)

`am-u09-bcbs-2000-wp4-early-warning` · 2000-12 (publication) · staff paper: Ranjana Sahajwala, Paul Van den Bergh, Basel Committee on Banking Supervision

_Disclaimer (PDF p. 4, series note):_ “The views expressed in the Working Papers are those of their authors and do not represent the official views of the Basel Committee, its member institutions or the BIS.”

BCBS Working Paper No. 4 of December 2000, by Ranjana Sahajwala and Paul Van den Bergh, describes the “supervisory risk assessment and early warning systems” of G10 supervisors that use “artificial intelligence” in software to replicate examiners’ financial analysis, and states that an effort is under way to combine the potential of artificial intelligence of software with expert human judgement to produce accurate risk assessments of banks.

> These frameworks are also termed “general expert systems” for their use as distinct, stand-alone risk assessment systems.
>
> — PDF p. 19, section on financial ratio and peer group analysis (bcbs-2000-wp4-early-warning; primary, supports)

> In addition, an increased effort is under way to combine the potential of artificial intelligence of software with expert human judgement in order to produce accurate risk assessments of banking institutions.
>
> — PDF p. 45, ‘Conclusions’ (bcbs-2000-wp4-early-warning; primary, supports)

> Supervisory authorities currently using the risk assessment and early warning systems covered by this paper find them useful in their ongoing risk-focused banking supervision process.
>
> — PDF p. 45, ‘Conclusions’ (bcbs-2000-wp4-early-warning; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ A staff working paper under the series disclaimer, surveying supervisors’ practice. “Artificial intelligence” here denotes expert systems and ratio-based software that replicate an examiner’s analysis, not machine learning; the paper also describes statistical models of bank condition. It describes use and effort under way, not a recommendation by the Committee.

_Cross-family check (follow-up 2026-11-04 (decision 11a, before 2016), 2026-11-04 A1.4, 2026-11-04 A2.13, 2026-11-04 B27):_ new record from the pass before 2016; takes the earliest-located role (correction). Second check (2026-11-04): earliest located text, staff voice, December 2000 wording date (landing page 27 December 2000) and expert-system meaning agreed; the locators (PDF pp. 19 and 45) verified. The checker’s own A1 locators were wrong (A1.4); no pack change.

#### FSB, Financial Stability Implications from FinTech (June 2017) — Articulates (Articulation, Earliest located use of the name)

`am-u09-fsb-2017-fintech-fs` · 2017-06-27 (publication) · institutional text: Financial Stability Board

The FSB report of 27 June 2017 on financial stability implications from FinTech states, in its case study on AI and machine learning, that key potential benefits include applications of AI and machine learning in supervision, and that AI and machine learning can aid supervision by identifying new relationships in data.

> Key potential benefits include various applications of AI and machine learning in supervision, internal audit and regulatory reporting, as well as the ability to better monitor risks.
>
> — Annex G, "Potential to support financial stability", PDF p. 60 (printed p. 56) (fsb-2017-fintech-fs; primary, supports)

> AI and machine learning can aid supervision by allowing the identification of new relationships in data, without the filter of pre-specified models.
>
> — Same paragraph, PDF p. 60 (printed p. 56) (fsb-2017-fintech-fs; primary, supports)

> There is also a close link with “SupTech,” or the use of FinTech by supervisory authorities.
>
> — Glossary, entry "RegTech", PDF p. 38 (fsb-2017-fintech-fs; primary, context). A glossary definition that does not mention AI; recorded only for the earliest located use of the name (audit, 2026-11-02).

_Qualification:_ A statement of what the text says, not evidence that the risk has materialised or that any authority has adopted a policy. The annex describes its findings as preliminary.

_Cross-family check (A1.9, follow-up 2026-11-04):_ earliest-located role moved to BCBS Working Paper No. 4, December 2000 (am-u09-bcbs-2000-wp4-early-warning), found in the pass before 2016; recorded as a correction. This report stays the earliest located institutional text and the earliest located use of “SupTech”. Bauguess (SEC) stays a lead (decision 10a).

#### Chakraborty and Joseph, BoE Staff Working Paper No. 674 (2017) — Articulates (Articulation)

`am-u09-boe-2017-swp674-ml-central-banks` · 2017-09 (publication) · staff paper: Chiranjit Chakraborty, Andreas Joseph, Bank of England

_Disclaimer (PDF p. 2 (abstract page), first footnote block):_ “The views expressed in this paper are those of the authors, and not necessarily those of the Bank of England or any of its committees.”

In Bank of England Staff Working Paper No. 674 of September 2017, Chiranjit Chakraborty and Andreas Joseph present a case study that models the detection of alerts on financial institutions’ balance sheets for banking supervision with machine-learning methods, which they write generally outperform traditional approaches in prediction.

> First, we model the detection of alerts on the balance sheets of financial institutions in the context of banking supervision.
>
> — PDF p. 2, Abstract (boe-2017-swp674-ml-central-banks; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ A staff working paper with the authors’ disclaimer; it demonstrates a possible supervisory use in a case study and does not describe a supervisory tool in operation.

#### FSB, AI and machine learning in financial services (November 2017) — Articulates (Articulation)

`am-u09-fsb-2017-ai-ml` · 2017-11-01 (publication) · institutional text: Financial Stability Board

The FSB report of 1 November 2017 defines SupTech as the use of these technologies by public sector regulators and supervisors and states that RegTech and SupTech applications of AI and machine learning can help increase supervisory effectiveness.

> The RegTech and SupTech applications of AI and machine learning can help improve regulatory compliance and increase supervisory effectiveness.
>
> — Executive summary, PDF p. 5 (printed p. 1) (fsb-2017-ai-ml; primary, supports)

> SupTech is the use of these technologies by public sector regulators and supervisors. Within SupTech, the objective of AI and machine learning applications is to enhance efficiency and effectiveness of supervision and surveillance.
>
> — Section 3.4, PDF p. 24 (printed p. 20) (fsb-2017-ai-ml; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ A statement of what the text says, not evidence that the risk has materialised or that any authority has adopted a policy. The June 2017 FSB report uses the word "SupTech" only in its glossary, without reference to AI. This November 2017 report uses the term together with AI. The earliest located use of the name in the pack is the June 2017 FSB report.

_Cross-family check (B77):_ corrected: qualification wording. Stance agreed.

#### BCBS, Sound Practices: implications of fintech developments (2018) — Articulates (Articulation)

`am-u09-bcbs-2018-sound-practices-fintech` · 2018-02 (publication) · institutional text: Basel Committee on Banking Supervision

The Basel Committee's 'Sound Practices' report of February 2018 states that technologies such as AI/ML and advanced data analytics may also have the potential to improve supervisory efficiency and effectiveness, and that supervisors exploring such technologies could enhance safety and soundness; it uses the term 'suptech'.

> The same technologies that offer efficiencies and opportunities for fintech firms and banks, such as AI/ML/advanced data analytics, DLT, cloud computing and APIs, may also have the potential to improve supervisory efficiency and effectiveness.
>
> — PDF p. 36 (printed p. 35), section C 'Suptech opportunities', Implication 8 (bcbs-2018-sound-practices-fintech; primary, supports)

>
> — Same page, Consideration 8 (bcbs-2018-sound-practices-fintech; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ Not earliest located in the pack; the report itself cites earlier texts. It describes supervisors' involvement with suptech as nascent and names lack of transparency of AI as a barrier.

#### FSI Insights No 9 (Broeders and Prenio, 2018) — Articulates (Articulation)

`am-u09-fsi-2018-insights-9` · 2018-07 (publication) · staff paper: Dirk Broeders, Jermy Prenio, Financial Stability Institute, Bank for International Settlements

_Disclaimer (PDF p. 2 (inside cover, series note)):_ “The views expressed in them are solely those of the authors and do not necessarily reflect those of the BIS or the Basel-based committees.”

FSI Insights No 9 of July 2018, by Dirk Broeders and Jermy Prenio, states that suptech applications, particularly in data analytics, are seen as capable of turning risk and compliance monitoring into a predictive and proactive process, and reports AI/ML applications at supervisory agencies, among them an anti-money-laundering network analysis at the Monetary Authority of Singapore.

> Suptech applications, particularly in the area of data analytics, are seen as capable of turning risk and compliance monitoring from a backward-looking into a predictive and proactive process.
>
> — PDF p. 4 (printed p. 1), executive summary (fsi-2018-insights-9; primary, supports)

> Using artificial intelligence (AI)/machine learning (ML) to do the same thing will require only a few minutes.
>
> — PDF p. 14, Box 3 'Natural language processing and machine learning at the Monetary Authority of Singapore' (fsi-2018-insights-9; primary, supports). The box relays the Monetary Authority of Singapore's description of its application.

_Qualification:_ A staff paper under the FSI series disclaimer, based on interviews with supervisory agencies; it reports expected benefits and names lack of transparency in some analytics applications as a critical issue.

#### Brainard, 'What Are We Learning about AI in Financial Services?' (2018) — Articulates (Articulation)

`am-u09-frb-2018-brainard-ai` · 2018-11-13 (delivery) · official’s speech or remarks: Lael Brainard (Governor (Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System)), Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System

In a speech of 13 November 2018, Lael Brainard, then a member of the Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System, said in a footnote that AI tools are likely to be useful for central banks and regulators in their responsibilities for supervision, financial stability and monetary policy, citing the FSB's 2017 report on uses ranging from systemic risk identification to detecting fraud and money laundering.

>
> — Footnote 8 (frb-2018-brainard-ai; primary, context). Relay of the FSB report.

_Qualification:_ A footnote that the speaker says is 'not addressed here'; the examples of supervisory uses are relayed from the FSB report. The speaker's view with disclaimer.

#### PBoC, FinTech Development Plan 2022–2025 (notice of 29 December 2021) — Articulates (Articulation)

`am-u09-pbc-2021-fintech-plan-2022-2025` · 2021-12-29 (signature) · institutional text: 中国人民银行 (People's Bank of China)

The People's Bank of China's FinTech Development Plan 2022–2025, issued by notice of 29 December 2021, sets the task of applying natural language processing, pattern recognition and related techniques to structure regulatory rules and compliance requirements, and of deepening the use of supervisory technology (监管科技) in areas including monetary policy, payments, anti-money laundering, credit reporting and consumer protection.

> 运用自然语言处理、模式识别等技术对监管规则、合规要求进行结构化处理
>
> — Attachment, item （二十二）加快监管科技全方位应用 (pbc-2021-fintech-plan-2022-2025; primary, supports). English paraphrase: use natural language processing, pattern recognition and other techniques to structure regulatory rules and compliance requirements.

#### McCaul, article for Revue Banque, 26 February 2024 — Articulates (Articulation)

`am-u09-ssm-2024-mccaul-data-to-decisions` · 2024-02-26 (publication) · official’s speech or remarks: Elizabeth McCaul (Member of the Supervisory Board of the ECB), European Central Bank (Banking Supervision)

In an article for Revue Banque of 26 February 2024, Elizabeth McCaul, Member of the Supervisory Board of the ECB, wrote that supervisors should use AI to enhance internal supervisory capabilities and gain insight into risks at banks that deploy AI, describing AI applications such as Athena and generative-AI proofs of concept.

>

> In the field of textual analysis, for example, our Athena application translates and analyses the content of supervisory documents.

_Qualification:_ The article also states that the tools do not replace supervisors and names the “black box” risk; it describes tools and intentions, not an evaluation of supervisory outcomes. No views disclaimer was found on the page.

#### IMF, GFSR October 2024, chapter 3 — Articulates (Articulation)

`am-u09-imf-2024-gfsr-ch3` · 2024-10-22 (publication) · institutional text: International Monetary Fund

_Disclaimer (eLibrary page header, "Legal-disclaimer"):_ “The views expressed in this publication are those of the IMF staff and do not necessarily represent the views of the IMF’s Executive Directors or their national authorities.”

Chapter 3 of the IMF Global Financial Stability Report of October 2024, prepared by IMF staff, states that AI provides opportunities for supervisors to automate data quality checks and help financial sector authorities detect anomalies in trading patterns.

> AI is providing numerous opportunities for supervisors to generate efficiency gains by automating data quality checks to ensure completeness, correctness, and consistency.
>
> — Section "Best Practices", paragraph beginning "AI is providing numerous opportunities" (HTML) (imf-2024-gfsr-ch3; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ A statement of what the text says, not evidence that the risk has materialised or that any authority has adopted a policy.

#### Federal Reserve Board, Financial Stability Report, November 2025, Box 1.1 — Articulates (Articulation)

`am-u09-frb-2025-fsr-nov` · 2025-11 (publication) · institutional text: Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System

The Federal Reserve Board's Financial Stability Report of November 2025 (Box 1.1) states that AI has the potential to significantly enhance market surveillance techniques for investigators and supervisors, and that major electronic market operators already use advanced machine learning to detect market manipulation and collusive behaviours.

> At the same time, however, AI has the potential to significantly enhance market surveillance techniques for investigators and supervisors. Major electronic market operators are already utilizing advanced machine learning techniques to detect market manipulation and collusive behaviors.
>
> — Box 1.1 'Artificial Intelligence and Algorithmic Trading', PDF p. 23 (printed p. 15), paragraph 4 (frb-2025-fsr-nov; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ A statement of potential in the Board's report; the box does not state that the Federal Reserve uses such tools. The current use it reports is by market operators.

### U10. AI can improve financial firms' risk management

**Criterion.** AI or machine-learning models can improve financial firms' measurement or management of risk, such as credit assessment, fraud detection, anti-money-laundering controls or market-risk modelling.

**Earliest located:** Phillips, 'Risk management' (Federal Reserve Board speech, 1997), 1997-11-04 (`am-u10-frb-1997-phillips-risk`).
**Earliest located institutional text (derived):** FSB, Financial Stability Implications from FinTech (June 2017), 2017-06-27 (`am-u10-fsb-2017-fintech-fs`).

#### Phillips, 'Risk management' (Federal Reserve Board speech, 1997) — Articulates (Earliest located)

`am-u10-frb-1997-phillips-risk` · 1997-11-04 (delivery) · official’s speech or remarks: Susan M. Phillips (Governor (Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System)), Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System

In remarks of 4 November 1997, Susan M. Phillips, Governor of the Federal Reserve Board, said that some institutions are making significant strides to better quantify and manage credit risk, including through credit scoring and the use of artificial intelligence in underwriting various types of consumer loans.

> At present, some institutions are making significant strides on a number of fronts to better quantify and manage credit risk. In addition to major developments in credit scoring and the use of artificial intelligence in underwriting various types of consumer loans
>
> — Section 'Increased Use of Internal Measurement and Management Systems', paragraph beginning 'Moving forward, perhaps such supervisory/private sector synergies' (frb-1997-phillips-risk; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ A single mention within a list of credit-risk developments; the speech does not describe how AI improves underwriting or assess its effect. Located through Governor Barr's citation of it (speech of 11 November 2025); a citation is not agreement or influence.

#### FSB, Financial Stability Implications from FinTech (June 2017) — Articulates (Articulation)

`am-u10-fsb-2017-fintech-fs` · 2017-06-27 (publication) · institutional text: Financial Stability Board

The FSB report of 27 June 2017 on financial stability implications from FinTech states that some firms use AI for anti-money-laundering and fraud detection, that firms can use machine learning for credit monitoring and risk mitigation, and that these applications can help institutions better manage risks.

> Some firms use AI for AML and fraud detection at financial institutions. Firms can use machine learning for credit monitoring, risk mitigation as well as fraud monitoring in banking.
>
> — Annex G, "Description", PDF p. 59 (printed p. 55) (fsb-2017-fintech-fs; primary, supports)

> These applications can help financial institutions, as well as supervisors, better understand causal relationships and better manage risks, and regulatory compliance.
>
> — Annex G, "Potential to support financial stability", PDF p. 60 (printed p. 56) (fsb-2017-fintech-fs; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ A statement of what the text says, not evidence that the risk has materialised or that any authority has adopted a policy. Descriptions of reported uses and possible benefits, not an assessment of performance.

#### FSB, AI and machine learning in financial services (November 2017) — Articulates (Articulation)

`am-u10-fsb-2017-ai-ml` · 2017-11-01 (publication) · institutional text: Financial Stability Board

The FSB report of 1 November 2017 states that AI and machine learning can be used for risk management through earlier and more accurate estimation of risks and for detecting fraud, suspicious transactions, default and cyber-attack risk, while noting that such tools remain untested under shifting financial conditions.

> AI and machine learning can be used for risk management through earlier and more accurate estimation of risks.
>
> — Section 4.2, item b), PDF p. 29 (printed p. 25) (fsb-2017-ai-ml; primary, supports)

> While AI and machine learning tools hold potential to improve risk management, the recent deployment of these strategies means that they remain untested at addressing risk under shifting financial conditions.
>
> — Section 4.2, item b), PDF p. 29 (printed p. 25) (fsb-2017-ai-ml; primary, context)

_Qualification:_ A statement of what the text says, not evidence that the risk has materialised or that any authority has adopted a policy.

#### BCBS, Sound Practices: implications of fintech developments (2018) — Articulates (Articulation)

`am-u10-bcbs-2018-sound-practices-fintech` · 2018-02 (publication) · institutional text: Basel Committee on Banking Supervision

The Basel Committee's 'Sound Practices' report of February 2018 states that analytics of big data associated with machine learning and AI can support banks' financial crime divisions in monitoring and reporting suspicious transactions.

>

#### BCBS, Sound Practices: implications of fintech developments (2018) — Bears on (Context)

`am-u10-bcbs-2018-sound-practices-fintech-context` · 2018-02 (publication) · institutional text: Basel Committee on Banking Supervision

The Basel Committee's 'Sound Practices' report of February 2018 states that the accuracy and validity of AI/ML models increasingly used for the termination of credit limits is as yet unproven.

_Missing:_ A statement that AI or machine-learning models do not, or cannot, improve risk management; the passage calls their accuracy and validity for the termination of credit limits unproven.

>
> — PDF p. 31 (printed p. 30), subsection 'Artificial intelligence/machine learning/advanced data analytics' (bcbs-2018-sound-practices-fintech; primary, supports). 'termination' as printed.

_Qualification:_ A limited dissent: it calls the models unproven, not inferior, and the same report articulates U10 for AML.

>
> — PDF p. 3 (printed p. 3) (boe-2019-proudman-managing-machines; primary, supports)

>
> — PDF p. 3 (printed p. 3) (boe-2019-proudman-managing-machines; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The speech relies in part on survey evidence from firms and an IIF survey; it also turns to the risks. Not an evaluation of firms’ risk outcomes.

#### BIS, Annual Economic Report 2019, chapter III — Articulates (Articulation)

`am-u10-bis-2019-aer-ch3` · 2019-06-30 (publication) · institutional text: Bank for International Settlements

The BIS Annual Economic Report 2019 (chapter III) states that an internal rating system based on machine learning and platform data can outperform simple models based on bureau scores in predicting defaults, while it is an open question whether it outperforms bank models and holds over full cycles.

>

#### PBoC, FinTech Development Plan 2019–2021 — Articulates (Articulation)

`am-u10-pbc-2019-fintech-plan` · 2019-08 (publication) · institutional text: 中国人民银行 (People's Bank of China)

The People's Bank of China's FinTech Development Plan 2019–2021 (August 2019) states that using big data, artificial intelligence and other technologies to build financial risk-control models can effectively identify high-risk transactions, sense abnormal transactions and enable early identification, alerting and handling of risks.

> 运用大数据、人工智能等技术建立金融风控模型，有效甄别高风险交易，智能感知异常交易，实现风险早识别、早预警、早处置，提升金融风险技防能力。
>
> — 第一章 发展形势, 第一节 重要意义, paragraph 金融科技成为防范化解金融风险的新利器, PDF p. 7 (printed p. 7) (pbc-2019-fintech-plan; primary, supports). English paraphrase: use big data, artificial intelligence and other technologies to build financial risk-control models that effectively identify high-risk transactions, intelligently sense abnormal transactions, achieve early identification, early warning and early handling of risks, and raise technological capacity to prevent financial risk.

_Qualification:_ A statement in a strategy document of what the technology can do, not evidence of results. English rendering is a paraphrase.

The Hong Kong Monetary Authority's circular of 9 September 2024 states that the HKMA observes that AI-powered suspicious-activity monitoring systems adopted by some authorized institutions have proved more effective and efficient than conventional rules-based transaction monitoring.

> These enhanced systems have proved to be more effective and efficient than conventional rules-based transaction monitoring systems commonly used by AIs.

#### BIS, Annual Economic Report 2025, chapter III — Articulates (Articulation)

`am-u10-bis-2025-aer-ch3` · 2025-06-29 (publication) · institutional text: Bank for International Settlements

The BIS Annual Economic Report 2025 (chapter III) states that machine learning tools present opportunities to help combat financial crime, reducing false positives and increasing detection of fraudulent transactions.

> AI and data integration advances, particularly through the development of machine learning tools, present opportunities to help combat financial crime. These innovations help reduce false positives (ie legitimate transactions flagged as illicit) and increase detection of fraudulent transactions.
>

### U11. Central banks need to build their own AI capacity

**Criterion.** Central banks need to invest in their own AI capabilities (data, computing, skills or staff), or to cooperate or pool resources with other central banks, to make use of AI or keep pace with its adoption.

**Earliest located:** BIS Working Papers No 930 (Doerr, Gambacorta and Serena, 2021), 2021-03 (`am-u11-bis-wp-930`).
**Earliest located institutional text (derived):** BIS, Annual Economic Report 2024, chapter III, 2024-06-30 (`am-u11-bis-2024-aer-ch3`).

#### BIS Working Papers No 930 (Doerr, Gambacorta and Serena, 2021) — Articulates (Earliest located)

`am-u11-bis-wp-930` · 2021-03 (publication) · staff paper: Sebastian Doerr, Leonardo Gambacorta, Jose Maria Serena, Bank for International Settlements

_Disclaimer (PDF p. 3, author note):_ “The views expressed are those of the authors and not necessarily those of the BIS.”

BIS Working Paper No 930 of March 2021, by Sebastian Doerr, Leonardo Gambacorta and Jose Maria Serena, reports that several central banks face constraints in IT infrastructure and human capital for big data and machine learning, and states that cooperation among public authorities could improve central banks' ability to collect, store and analyse big data.

> Several institutions report constraints in setting up an adequate IT infrastructure and in developing the necessary human capital. Cooperation among public authorities could improve central banks' ability to collect, store and analyse big data.
>
> — PDF p. 3, abstract (bis-wp-930; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ A staff working paper under its disclaimer, drawing on an IFC survey. Its subject is big data used with machine learning, not AI generally.

#### IMF Departmental Paper 2021/024 (Boukherouaa, Shabsigh and others) — Bears on (Context)

`am-u11-imf-2021-dp-ai-finance-context` · 2021-10-22 (publication) · staff paper: El Bachir Boukherouaa, Ghiath Shabsigh, Khaled AlAjmi, Jose Deodoro, Aquiles Farias, Ebru S. Iskender, Alin T. Mirestean, Rangachary Ravikumar, International Monetary Fund

_Disclaimer (PDF p. 3, series note):_ “The views expressed in this paper are those of the author(s) and do not necessarily represent the views of the IMF, its Executive Board, or IMF management.”

In the IMF Departmental Paper of 22 October 2021, Boukherouaa, AlAjmi, Deodoro, Farias and Ravikumar write that central banks have been slow to adopt AI/ML, attributing this in part to a lack of adequate capacity, and that risks would be amplified if a central bank lacks the resources and skills to operate AI/ML safely.

_Missing:_ An explicit statement that central banks need to invest in, or pool, AI capabilities; the conclusion’s call for capacity building is addressed to regulators generally.

> central banks’ slow adoption thereof can be attributed to cultural, political, and legal factors, and lack of adequate capacity
>
> — Chapter 2, section E "Central Banking" (HTML) (imf-2021-dp-ai-finance; primary, supports)

> These concerns would be amplified if the central bank does not have the resources and skills required to safely operate AI/ML or mitigate the risks associated with it.
>
> — Chapter 2, section E (HTML) (imf-2021-dp-ai-finance; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ A staff paper attributed to its authors. The text describes capacity as a constraint and a risk factor; it does not state that central banks need to invest in their own AI capabilities or pool resources.

In a speech of 29 November 2021, Pentti Hakkarainen, Member of the Supervisory Board of the ECB, said that supervisory staff must be given the opportunity to build the skills to operate suptech tools, describing an AI training programme for more than 500 supervisors and a suptech team established at the ECB.

>

>
> — Paragraph beginning “First, we are making a sustained effort” (ssm-2021-reaping-suptech; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ Concerns ECB Banking Supervision and national supervisors in the SSM; the AI element is the training programme and NLP tools. A statement of need and of steps taken, as given by the speaker.

In a speech of 22 November 2024, Michelle W. In a speech of 11 November 2025, Michael S. Barr, Governor of the Federal Reserve Board, said that central banks, including the Fed, need to keep up with AI by increasing their speed of adoption for their own operations, and described the Federal Reserve's AI programme, 'hands on keys' staff engagement and use of generative AI for technology modernization.

#### Tao Zhang, 'AI, monetary policy and financial stability' (2026) — Articulates (Articulation)

`am-u11-bis-2026-zhang-ai-monpol` · 2026-10-20 (delivery) · official’s speech or remarks: Tao Zhang (BIS Chief Representative for Asia and the Pacific), Bank for International Settlements

_Disclaimer (BIS page, note at the end of the text):_ “The views expressed here are my own and not necessarily those of the BIS or its member institutions.”

### U12. Existing financial rules broadly cover AI

**Criterion.** Existing financial regulatory and supervisory frameworks (for example technology-neutral rules on model risk, governance, outsourcing or consumer protection) broadly apply to the use of AI by financial firms, so that AI does not, or does not yet, require a separate regulatory framework.

**Search terms (not names).** technology-neutral; technology neutral.

**Earliest located:** Brainard, 'What Are We Learning about AI in Financial Services?' (2018), 2018-11-13 (`am-u12-frb-2018-brainard-ai`).
**Cross-family check on the headline (A1.12, R4, follow-up 2026-11-04, 2026-11-04 A1.5, 2026-11-04 A2.16, 2026-11-04 B24, 2026-11-04 C28, 2026-11-04 C29):** The earliest located institutional text for U12 moves from DP5/22 (11 October 2022) to this paper (25 July 2019), as the checker found (A1.12). Brainard (13 November 2018) stays the earliest located text.
**Earliest located institutional text (derived):** DNB, General principles for the use of AI in the financial sector (2019), 2019-07-25 (`am-u12-dnb-2019-ai-principles`).
**Earliest located contesting text:** PBoC, FinTech Development Plan 2019–2021, 2019-08 (`am-u12-pbc-2019-fintech-plan-contests`).

#### FSB, AI and machine learning in financial services (November 2017) — Bears on (Context)

`am-u12-fsb-2017-ai-ml-context` · 2017-11-01 (publication) · institutional text: Financial Stability Board

The FSB report of 1 November 2017 states that there were no known dedicated international standards on AI and machine learning, and that, as the current regulatory framework was not designed with such tools in mind, some regulatory practices may need revising for their benefits to be harnessed.

_Missing:_ A statement on whether existing frameworks broadly apply to AI or whether AI requires a separate framework.

> Because AI and machine learning applications are relatively new, there are no known dedicated international standards in this area.
>
> — Section 4.4, PDF p. 32 (printed p. 28) (fsb-2017-ai-ml; primary, supports)

> as the current regulatory framework is not designed with the use of such tools in mind, some regulatory practices may need to be revised for the benefits of AI and machine learning techniques to be fully harnessed.
>
> — Section 5.4, PDF p. 36 (printed p. 32) (fsb-2017-ai-ml; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The passage on revising practices concerns firms’ and supervisors’ use of AI for compliance (RegTech and SupTech), not a judgement on whether existing rules cover the risks of AI.

#### Brainard, 'What Are We Learning about AI in Financial Services?' (2018) — Articulates (Earliest located)

`am-u12-frb-2018-brainard-ai` · 2018-11-13 (delivery) · official’s speech or remarks: Lael Brainard (Governor (Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System)), Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System

_Disclaimer (Footnote 1):_ “These remarks represent my own views, which do not necessarily represent those of the Federal Reserve Board or the Federal Open Market Committee.”

In a speech of 13 November 2018, Lael Brainard, then a member of the Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System, said that existing regulatory and supervisory guardrails are a good place to start in assessing the approach to AI, naming the Federal Reserve's model risk management guidance (SR 11-7) and vendor risk management guidance as relevant to AI tools.

> Our existing regulatory and supervisory guardrails are a good place to start as we assess the appropriate approach for AI processes.
>
> — Section 'Current Regulatory and Supervisory Approaches', paragraph beginning 'It is incumbent on regulators' (frb-2018-brainard-ai; primary, supports)

> could be expected to apply as well to AI-based tools or services that are externally sourced.
>
> — Same section, paragraph beginning 'Second, our guidance on vendor risk management' (frb-2018-brainard-ai; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ A starting point rather than a conclusion: she also says the Federal Reserve is 'still learning' and welcomes discussion of how existing laws and guidance intersect with AI. The speaker's view with disclaimer, not Board guidance.

#### Proudman, speech of 4 June 2019 — Articulates (Articulation)

`am-u12-boe-2019-proudman-managing-machines` · 2019-06-04 (delivery) · official’s speech or remarks: James Proudman (Executive Director of UK Deposit Takers Supervision), Bank of England

In a speech of 4 June 2019, James Proudman, Executive Director of UK Deposit Takers Supervision at the Bank of England, said that the regulatory reforms of recent years, developed for human problems in workplaces, remain equally relevant to an AI/ML-centric workplace, with firms needing to allocate responsibilities under the Senior Managers Regime.

> It follows that the regulatory reforms over recent years were developed to overcome the very ‘human’ problems embodied in people-centric workplaces … remain equally relevant to an AI/ML-centric workplace.
>
> — PDF p. 6 (printed p. 6) (boe-2019-proudman-managing-machines; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The passage concerns governance and accountability rules; the speaker also says it may become harder to attribute accountability. It does not address whether AI-specific rules are needed elsewhere.

#### DNB, General principles for the use of AI in the financial sector (2019) — Articulates (Articulation)

`am-u12-dnb-2019-ai-principles` · 2019-07-25 (publication) · institutional text: De Nederlandsche Bank

De Nederlandsche Bank’s discussion paper of 25 July 2019 on general principles for the use of AI in the financial sector, which presents DNB’s preliminary view, states that many of the prerequisites for a responsible application of AI are already covered by existing regulations, in particular the requirements on sound and controlled business operations.

> De Nederlandsche Bank (DNB) presents its preliminary view on a set of possible principles in this discussion paper.
>
> — PDF p. 5, ‘Practical information’ (dnb-2019-ai-principles; primary, context)

> On the one hand, this implies that many of the prerequisites for a responsible application of AI are already covered by existing regulations. In particular the requirements regarding sound and controlled business operations (see Financial Supervision Act articles 3:10, 3:17 and 3:18 for legal context) cover many aspects.
>
> — PDF p. 33, chapter 4 ‘General principles for the use of AI by financial institutions (‘SAFEST’)’ (dnb-2019-ai-principles; primary, supports)

> Moreover, loopholes in regulation may exist and societal expectations may go beyond what current regulations require.
>
> — PDF p. 33, same paragraph (dnb-2019-ai-principles; primary, context)

_Qualification:_ A discussion paper of preliminary views inviting comment, not final policy. The same passage states that loopholes in regulation may exist, that societal expectations may go beyond current rules, and that legislators and supervisors are considering AI-specific policies or regulations; it does not state that no separate framework is needed. The paper names an author, Joost van der Burgt (PDF p. 2), and presents DNB’s view; the English wording is not authenticated against a July 2019 capture.

#### PBoC, FinTech Development Plan 2019–2021 — Contests (Earliest located)

`am-u12-pbc-2019-fintech-plan-contests` · 2019-08 (publication) · institutional text: 中国人民银行 (People's Bank of China)

The People's Bank of China's FinTech Development Plan 2019–2021 (August 2019) sets tasks to research and formulate regulatory rules for AI financial applications and to promote the establishment of laws and regulations, ethical norms and a policy system for AI financial applications.

_Engagement:_ contrary-only. The plan calls for AI-specific regulatory rules and laws for financial applications of AI without discussing whether existing financial rules already apply.

> 健全人工智能金融应用安全监测预警机制，研究制定人工智能金融应用监管规则，强化智能化金融工具安全认证
>
> — 第三章 重点任务, 第二节, item （六）稳步应用人工智能, PDF p. 14 (printed p. 14) (pbc-2019-fintech-plan; primary, supports). English paraphrase: improve the security monitoring and early-warning mechanism for AI financial applications, research and formulate regulatory rules for AI financial applications, and strengthen security certification of intelligent financial tools.

> 推动建立人工智能金融应用法律法规、伦理规范和政策体系
>
> — Same item, PDF p. 14 (pbc-2019-fintech-plan; primary, supports). English paraphrase: promote the establishment of laws and regulations, ethical norms and a policy system for AI financial applications.

_Qualification:_ Planned tasks, not adopted rules; the text does not state that existing rules are insufficient. English renderings are paraphrases.

#### BaFin and Bundesbank, Machine learning in risk models (consultation paper, July 2021) — Articulates (Articulation)

The joint consultation paper of BaFin and the Deutsche Bundesbank of 15 July 2021 on machine learning in risk models states that, since the current regulatory foundations are worded in a technology-neutral manner, they are largely transferable to the characteristics of machine-learning methods, with only a few places where adaptation might be necessary.

> since the current regulatory foundations are worded in a technology-neutral manner, they are largely transferable to the characteristics of ML methods, with only a few places where it might be necessary to adapt the regulatory foundations.
>
> — PDF p. 4, chapter I ‘Machine learning is advancing’ (buba-2021-ml-risk-models-consultation; primary, supports)

>
> — Section ‘Consultation 11/2021’ (buba-ml-methods-page; primary, context)

_Qualification:_ A consultation paper on risk models of banks and insurers (Pillars 1 and 2), inviting comment; it states the authorities’ view at that date, not final policy, and names a few places where adaptation might be necessary.

#### FSI Insights No 35 (Prenio and Yong, 2021) — Articulates (Articulation)

`am-u12-fsi-2021-insights-35` · 2021-08 (publication) · staff paper: Jermy Prenio, Jeffery Yong, Financial Stability Institute, Bank for International Settlements

_Disclaimer (PDF p. 2 (inside cover, series note)):_ “The views expressed in them are solely those of the authors and do not necessarily reflect those of the BIS or the Basel-based committees.”

FSI Insights No 35 of August 2021, by Jermy Prenio and Jeffery Yong, states that existing requirements on governance, risk management and traditional models also apply to AI models, and that most issues arising from AI use are similar to those for traditional models.

> Existing requirements on governance, risk management, as well as development and operation of traditional models also apply to AI models.
>
> — PDF p. 4 (printed p. 1), executive summary (fsi-2021-insights-35; primary, supports)

> While most of the issues arising from the use of AI by financial institutions are similar to those for traditional models, the perspective might be different.
>
> — PDF p. 4 (printed p. 1), executive summary (fsi-2021-insights-35; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ A staff paper under the FSI series disclaimer. The same summary also states that there is scope for standard-setting bodies to develop international guidance on AI and calls for a proportional regulatory response.

#### IMF Departmental Paper 2021/024 (Boukherouaa, Shabsigh and others) — Bears on (Context)

`am-u12-imf-2021-dp-ai-finance-context` · 2021-10-22 (publication) · staff paper: El Bachir Boukherouaa, Ghiath Shabsigh, Khaled AlAjmi, Jose Deodoro, Aquiles Farias, Ebru S. Iskender, Alin T. Mirestean, Rangachary Ravikumar, International Monetary Fund

_Disclaimer (PDF p. 3, series note):_ “The views expressed in this paper are those of the author(s) and do not necessarily represent the views of the IMF, its Executive Board, or IMF management.”

In the IMF Departmental Paper of 22 October 2021, Boukherouaa and Shabsigh, with AlAjmi, Deodoro, Farias, Iskender, Mirestean and Ravikumar, write that regulatory gaps could affect financial stability if technological advances outpace existing regulations, and that an adequate policy response requires clear minimum standards and guidelines for the sector; they report that some jurisdictions have concluded existing regulations are sufficient.

_Missing:_ The paper calls for clear minimum standards and guidelines, guidance on explainability and an expanded cybersecurity perimeter, and states that regulatory gaps could matter if technological advances outpace existing regulations; it reports that some jurisdictions found existing regulations sufficient and that regulators focus on governance and risk management “by way of new regulations or existing ones”. It does not state that existing frameworks broadly fail to cover AI or that a separate framework is needed.

> regulatory gaps could adversely impact financial stability if technological advances outpace existing regulations.
>
> — Chapter 3, section F, final bullet (HTML) (imf-2021-dp-ai-finance; primary, context)

> An adequate policy response requires developing clear minimum standards and guidelines for the sector, coupled with stronger focus on securing the necessary technical skills.
>
> — Chapter 3, section F (HTML) (imf-2021-dp-ai-finance; primary, context)

> others have concluded that existing regulations and expectations on good governance are sufficient to address the emerging issues.
>
> — Chapter 3, section F (HTML) (imf-2021-dp-ai-finance; primary, context)

_Qualification:_ A staff paper attributed to its authors. Re-read under decision 18a: calls for standards and guidance in specific areas, which do not alone contest broad coverage; the paper states no view on whether existing frameworks broadly cover AI.

_Cross-family check (decision 18a, follow-up 2026-11-04, 2026-11-04 A2.11):_ re-read: contests to bears on (context). Former id am-u12-imf-2021-dp-ai-finance-contests. Second check (2026-11-04): bears on agreed under decision 18a; the checker verified the passages in the official IMF PDF (Chapter 3, section F, printed p. 19, PDF p. 22). Authors corrected from the PDF cover, read here (the same bytes as the checker’s copy).

`am-u12-boe-2022-dp5-22` · 2022-10-11 (publication) · institutional text: Bank of England (including the PRA) and Financial Conduct Authority

The Bank of England (including the PRA) and the Financial Conduct Authority state in the foreword to DP5/22 of 11 October 2022 that, given the overlap of existing sectoral rules that apply to AI, their approach is largely limited to clarifying how the existing framework applies to AI and addressing identified gaps.

#### Knot, The AI adventure (FSB, 11 July 2024) — Bears on (Context)

`am-u12-fsb-2024-knot-ai-page-context` · 2024-07-11 (delivery) · official’s speech or remarks: Klaas Knot (Chair, Financial Stability Board), Financial Stability Board

_Disclaimer (Head of the speech page, below the title):_ “The views expressed in these remarks are those of the speaker in his role as FSB Chair and do not necessarily reflect those of the FSB or its members.”

In a speech of 11 July 2024, Klaas Knot, Chair of the Financial Stability Board, said that many potential AI risks resemble traditional financial risks, that frameworks already exist to assess concentration risk, third-party dependence and interconnectedness, and that regulators are not starting from scratch.

> — Speech text, paragraph beginning "Many of the potential risks" (fsb-2024-knot-ai-page; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ The speaker’s view in his role as FSB Chair (disclaimer recorded). He also said the FSB was ready to implement effective regulatory frameworks; he does not say whether AI requires a separate framework.

Chapter 3 of the IMF Global Financial Stability Report of October 2024, prepared by IMF staff, states that existing regulatory and supervisory frameworks for capital markets are largely technology-neutral and applicable to AI systems, and that many AI risks are addressed by existing frameworks, while recommending additional policy responses.

_Qualification:_ The chapter’s scope is capital markets. It also states that authorities should consider additional policy responses and question whether extant frameworks adapt to novel forms of AI; the record does not show that no new rules are needed.

#### FSI Insights No 63 (Crisanto, Leuterio, Prenio and Yong, 2024) — Bears on (Context)

`am-u12-fsi-2024-insights-63-context` · 2024-12 (publication) · staff paper: Juan Carlos Crisanto, Cris Benson Leuterio, Jermy Prenio, Jeffery Yong, Financial Stability Institute, Bank for International Settlements

_Disclaimer (PDF p. 2 (inside cover, series note)):_ “The views expressed in them are solely those of the authors and do not necessarily reflect those of the BIS or the Basel-based committees.”

FSI Insights No 63 of December 2024, by Crisanto, Leuterio, Prenio and Yong, also states that AI use may present unique challenges so that regulatory or supervisory guidance may be needed in specific areas, and that provider concentration strengthens the argument for direct oversight frameworks for cloud and AI service providers.

_Missing:_ The passages say that guidance may be needed in specific areas and that provider concentration strengthens the case for direct oversight of cloud and AI service providers; they do not dispute that existing rules broadly cover financial institutions’ use of AI, and the same summary states that they do (see the articulating record).

The EIOPA Opinion on AI governance and risk management of 6 August 2025 states that AI systems in insurance that are neither prohibited nor high-risk under the AI Act continue to operate under existing sectoral legislation without new requirements, and that the Opinion sets out no new requirements but guidance on interpreting existing insurance legislation for AI systems.

>
> — Section 2, paragraph 2.5, PDF p. 3 (eiopa-2025-ai-opinion; primary, supports)
