#### RBI FREE-AI Committee report (2025) — Bears on (Context)

`am-u12-rbi-2025-free-ai-report-context` · 2025-08-13 (publication) · committee report published by the authority: Pushpak Bhattacharyya (Chairperson), Debjani Ghosh, Balaraman Ravindran, Abhishek Singh, Rahul Matthan, Anjani Rathor, Sree Hari Nagaralu, Suvendu Pati (Member Secretary), Reserve Bank of India (Committee to develop a Framework for Responsible and Ethical Enablement of Artificial Intelligence in the Financial Sector)

The report of the FREE-AI Committee placed on the Reserve Bank of India’s website in August 2025 states that existing regulations need to incorporate certain incremental AI aspects to be comprehensive, and sets as its objective a framework for responsible and ethical AI adoption, recommending a dedicated institutional framework in the financial sector to issue financial sector–specific standards, audit benchmarks and guidance.

_Missing:_ The committee asks for incremental additions to existing regulations in named areas and for amendments “where existing regulations fail to adequately cover AI-specific risks”, and recommends a dedicated institutional framework (a standing committee and an AI-safety body) to assess risks, coordinate and issue standards and guidance. It does not state that existing frameworks broadly fail to cover AI or that a separate regulatory framework is needed.

> Despite the above existing regulations, there are certain incremental AI aspects that the existing regulations need to incorporate to make them comprehensive, such as AI-related disclosures, due diligence of vendors on AI risks, opportunities and risks in cybersecurity, etc.
>
> — Chapter 3, paragraph 3.2.3.5, PDF p. 34 (rbi-2025-free-ai-report; primary, context)

> Where existing regulations fail to adequately cover AI-specific risks, review and amendments of guidelines should be considered.
>
> — Chapter 4, recommendations on policy, PDF p. 55 (rbi-2025-free-ai-report; primary, context)

> The Committee’s overarching objective is therefore to establish a forward-looking and balanced framework for responsible and ethical AI adoption.
>
> — Chapter 4, paragraph 4.1.5, PDF p. 44 (rbi-2025-free-ai-report; primary, context)

> the creation of a dedicated institutional framework within the financial sector is needed to continuously assess AI-related risks, support cross-sectoral coordination, issue financial sector–specific standards, audit benchmarks, and guidance to promote responsible innovation.
>
> — Chapter 4, paragraph 4.4.33, PDF p. 59 (rbi-2025-free-ai-report; primary, context)

_Qualification:_ Second check (2026-11-04, A2.6), the checker’s reading agreed: the dedicated institutional framework of paragraph 4.4.33 is an institution for risk assessment, coordination and standard-setting, not a separate regulatory regime; with the incremental additions and conditional amendments (decision 18a) it does not contest broad coverage. The report also recommends guiding regulated entities on how existing regulations apply to AI. The committee’s text, not stated by the RBI to be its policy.

_Cross-family check (decision 18a, follow-up 2026-11-04, 2026-11-04 A2.6):_ contests to bears on (context), the checker’s reading (second check, A2.6), agreed by the preparer; no earliest-located role or headline is affected (U12’s earliest located contesting text stays PBoC 2019). Former id am-u12-rbi-2025-free-ai-report-contests.

#### FSB, Monitoring Adoption of AI and Related Vulnerabilities (October 2025) — Articulates (Later restatement)

`am-u12-fsb-2025-ai-monitoring` · 2025-10-10 (publication) · institutional text: Financial Stability Board · relay

The FSB report of 10 October 2025 on monitoring AI adoption reports that the member authorities interviewed indicated a preference to maintain technology-neutral approaches and to rely on existing policy frameworks unless AI-specific frameworks become necessary, while noting a tension with assessing AI-specific vulnerabilities.

> the authorities that were interviewed indicated a preference to maintain technology-neutral approaches and plan to rely … on existing policy frameworks unless the development of AI-specific frameworks becomes necessary.
>
> — Section 3.2, PDF pp. 16–17 (printed pp. 12–13) (fsb-2025-ai-monitoring; primary, supports). The ellipsis marks only the page break between PDF pp. 16 and 17 (page number); no words of the sentence are omitted.

> Some authorities also noted the tension between maintaining a technology-neutral approach and assessing AIspecific vulnerabilities.
>
> — Section 3.2, PDF p. 16 (printed p. 12) (fsb-2025-ai-monitoring; primary, context)

_Qualification:_ A relay: the report records the views of interviewed member authorities, not a conclusion of its own.

#### ESRB ASC Report No 16, AI and systemic risk (Cecchetti and others, December 2025) — Contests (Contesting)

`am-u12-esrb-2025-asc-ai-contests` · 2025-12 (publication) · committee report published by the authority: Stephen Cecchetti, Robin L. Lumsdaine, Tuomas Peltonen, Antonio Sánchez Serrano, European Systemic Risk Board

_Disclaimer (PDF p. 67, imprint page, "Note"):_ “The views expressed in the Reports of the Advisory Scientific Committee are those of the authors and do not necessarily reflect the official stance of the ESRB or its member organisations.”

In ESRB Advisory Scientific Committee Report No 16 of December 2025, Cecchetti, Lumsdaine, Peltonen and Sánchez Serrano write that AI does not necessitate a full overhaul of the regulatory system but that it is almost surely necessary to consider significant reforms, and that risks from AI provider concentration, model uniformity and cyber-attacks may require fundamental changes beyond the current prudential framework.

_Engagement:_ engages-premise. Disputes the premise that the current prudential framework suffices, stating that specific AI risks may require fundamental changes beyond it.

> While, in general, we believe that the advent of AI does not necessitate a full overhaul of the existing regulatory system, it is almost surely necessary to consider significant reforms.
>
> — Section 4.1, PDF p. 44 (printed p. 43) (esrb-2025-asc-ai; primary, supports)

> Addressing risks arising from things like the concentration in AI providers, model uniformity or the potential increase in cyber-attacks may require fundamental changes beyond the current prudential framework.
>
> — Section 4.2, PDF p. 45 (printed p. 44) (esrb-2025-asc-ai; primary, supports)

> While they may require recalibration, our view is that existing policy tools are likely sufficient to ensure financial system resilience as use of AI becomes ubiquitous in finance.
>
> — Section 4.2, PDF p. 44 (printed p. 43) (esrb-2025-asc-ai; primary, context)

_Qualification:_ An Advisory Scientific Committee report attributed to its authors, not the ESRB’s stance. The authors also write that existing policy tools are likely sufficient if recalibrated; the record holds their statements that significant reforms need to be considered and that some AI risks may require changes beyond the current prudential framework.

_Cross-family check (B52):_ corrected: modality restored ("necessary to consider significant reforms"). Stance agreed: contests, with the authors’ statement that existing tools are likely sufficient kept as evidence.

#### Machado, keynote speech, 24 February 2026 — Bears on (Context)

`am-u12-ssm-2026-machado-ai-governance-context` · 2026-02-24 (delivery) · official’s speech or remarks: Pedro Machado (ECB representative to the Supervisory Board (the page prints “ECB representative to the the Supervisory Board”)), European Central Bank (Banking Supervision)

In a keynote speech of 24 February 2026, Pedro Machado, ECB representative to the Supervisory Board, said that ECB Banking Supervision’s approach is technology-neutral but that AI introduces qualitative changes that cannot be addressed simply by expanding existing frameworks, which do not always fully cater to AI-specific challenges.

_Missing:_ Neither holds that existing frameworks broadly suffice for AI nor that a separate AI framework is needed; it states both technology neutrality and gaps.

> supervisors have also observed that AI introduces qualitative changes that cannot be addressed simply by expanding the existing frameworks.
>
> — Paragraph beginning “At the same time, supervisors have also observed” (ssm-2026-machado-ai-governance; primary, supports)

> Our approach is technology-neutral. We do not supervise technologies.
>
> — Paragraph beginning “Our approach is technology-neutral” (ssm-2026-machado-ai-governance; primary, supports)

_Qualification:_ Holds both a technology-neutral approach and gaps in existing frameworks; it calls for refined supervisory assessment, not new AI-specific regulation.

#### Breeden, speech published 30 June 2026 — Bears on (Context)

`am-u12-boe-2026-breeden-agents-of-change-context` · 2026-06-30 (publication) · official’s speech or remarks: Sarah Breeden (Deputy Governor, Financial Stability), Bank of England

In a speech published on 30 June 2026, Sarah Breeden, Deputy Governor for Financial Stability at the Bank of England, said that, as AI capabilities increase, “we must keep asking” whether existing, technology-agnostic regulatory frameworks remain sufficient, that they were not built to contemplate autonomous agents, and that more sophisticated governance and accountability frameworks may be needed.

_Missing:_ The speaker states that existing frameworks were not built to contemplate autonomous agents and that more sophisticated governance and accountability frameworks “may be needed”, framed as a question to keep asking about agents; she does not state that existing frameworks fail to cover AI broadly or that a separate AI framework is needed.

> as AI capabilities increase, we must keep asking whether existing, technology-agnostic regulatory frameworks remain sufficient. Our frameworks were not built to contemplate autonomous agents, and relying on a human in the loop for all agent actions is unlikely to be realistic.
>
> — Section “Agentic payments and commerce”, final paragraph (boe-2026-breeden-agents-of-change; primary, context)

_Qualification:_ Re-read under decision 18a: a question and a possible adaptation for autonomous agents, not a contest of broad coverage. She does not propose specific AI-specific rules. Compare her speech of 31 October 2024 (am-u12-boe-2024-breeden-engaging-machine). The cross-family checker (second check, A2.8) reads the passage as a qualified, prospective contest of continuing sufficiency for autonomous agents (frameworks not built for them; a human in the loop unlikely to be realistic), while noting that the pack’s distinction is reasonable and that the next sentence allows adapted governance frameworks. The record keeps that uncertainty.

_Cross-family check (decision 18a, follow-up 2026-11-04, 2026-11-04 A2.8):_ re-read: contests to bears on (context). Former id am-u12-boe-2026-breeden-agents-of-change-contests. Second check (2026-11-04, A2.8): the checker’s blind answer is a qualified prospective contest; its comparison accepts the pack’s reading under decision 18a and asks that the uncertainty be kept, not that the classification be overridden. Kept as bears on, with the checker’s reading in the qualification; no earliest-located role or headline is affected.

#### FSB, Sound Practices for Responsible Adoption of AI: Final report (October 2026) — Bears on (Context)

`am-u12-fsb-2026-ai-sound-practices-context` · 2026-10-15 (publication) · institutional text: Financial Stability Board

The FSB final report of 15 October 2026 on sound practices for responsible AI adoption states that the FSB and relevant standard-setting bodies emphasise proportionate, risk-based and technology-neutral approaches, and that its sound practices are non-binding and can be integrated into existing frameworks.

_Missing:_ The passages state technology-neutral, proportionate approaches and that the sound practices are non-binding; they do not state that existing financial regulatory and supervisory frameworks broadly cover AI. Where the report speaks of existing frameworks (PDF pp. 6 and 37), it means financial institutions’ own risk frameworks, which they adapt to AI.

> The FSB and relevant SSBs emphasise proportionate, risk‑based and technology‑neutral approaches to monitoring and addressing risks
>
> — Section 1.1, PDF p. 9 (printed p. 5) (fsb-2026-ai-sound-practices; primary, context)

> The sound practices are non-prescriptive and do not constitute a binding international standard.
>
> — Executive summary, PDF p. 6 (printed p. 2) (fsb-2026-ai-sound-practices; primary, context)

_Qualification:_ Recorded as context after the cross-family check (A2.30). The report also notes that some standard-setting bodies have issued AI-specific guidance and that some risks of agentic AI are newer; the record does not show that no AI-specific rules exist or are needed.

_Cross-family check (A2.30):_ corrected: articulates (later restatement) to bears on (context), as the checker found. Former id am-u12-fsb-2026-ai-sound-practices.

## Institutional events

Each event records what the authority’s own text states, with its stage. Announced stays announced; no start, completion or operation is inferred.

No start or completion date is stated.

The June 2017 report mentions non-financial firms offering AI applications as a regulatory challenge, without concentration.
- **U03** (international). Same texts, grepped for cyber*, fraud, deepfake*, manipulat*, impersonat*. _Earliest:_ The FSB report of 1 November 2017 is the earliest text found that links automated trading strategies to manipulation by cybercriminals and calls for AI uses to be assessed for cybersecurity risk. The IMF Departmental Paper of 2021 is the earliest text found that states AI/ML adoption itself increases the scope of cyber threats.
- **U04** (international). Same texts plus EBA Report on Big Data and Advanced Analytics (January 2020) and EBA follow-up report on ML for IRB models (August 2023), grepped for black box, explainab*, interpretab*, auditab*, opaque, opacity. _Earliest:_ The FSB report of 27 June 2017 (Annex G) is the earliest text found that states the opacity and lack of auditability of "black box" AI models as a key risk, including for model validation by regulators.
- **U05** (international). FSB 2017 November report section 3.4; IMF Departmental Paper 2021/024 chapter 2 section E and Box 6; grepped nowcast*, forecast*, central bank*. _Earliest:_ The FSB report of 1 November 2017 is the earliest text found in this group that states machine-learning and NLP tools may help authorities predict market and macroeconomic variables. Central bank texts are searched by other groups.
- **U06** (international). FSB Chair speech of 11 July 2024 (productivity and labour markets, no monetary-policy link); FSB 2024 report section on macroeconomic conditions; IMF GFSR chapter 3. No IMF speeches retrievable. _Earliest:_ No text in this group meets the criterion; the Knot speech discusses productivity and labour markets without monetary policy.
- **U07** (international). FSB 2025 monitoring report and 2026 sound practices grepped for agentic, agent*, payment*, commerce; IMF GFSR chapter 3 for autonomous agents. _Earliest:_ No text in this group meets the criterion. The GFSR 2024 chapter mentions interactions between autonomous AI agents in markets, not payments.
- **U08** (international). Same texts grepped for stablecoin*, tokeni*, programmable. _Earliest:_ No text in this group meets the criterion.
- **U09** (international). All texts grepped for SupTech, suptech, supervis* near AI or machine learning. _Earliest:_ The FSB report of 27 June 2017 (Annex G) is the earliest text found that states AI and machine learning can aid supervision. The FSB report of 1 November 2017 holds the earliest located use of the term "SupTech" together with AI within this group; the June 2017 report uses the term only in its glossary, without AI.
- **U10** (international). All texts grepped for risk management, fraud, AML, credit scor*, predictive power. _Earliest:_ The FSB report of 27 June 2017 (Annex G) is the earliest text found that states firms use AI and machine learning for AML, fraud detection and credit monitoring and that these help them manage risks.
- **U11** (international). IMF Departmental Paper 2021/024 section E; FSB 2024 report section 4.1 on authorities’ skills; ESRB ASC report section 4.3; grepped capacity, skills, resources. _Earliest:_ No text in this group fully meets the criterion; the IMF Departmental Paper of 2021 is recorded as context. The FSB 2024 report states that inadequate investment by financial authorities in skills and resources could pose risks (authorities generally, not central banks).
- **U12** (international). All texts grepped for technology-neutral, technology neutral, existing (regulat*|framework*|legislation|rules), new requirements, AI-specific; FSB Chair speech and letter; EIOPA Opinion 2025. _Earliest:_ Chapter 3 of the IMF GFSR of October 2024 is the earliest text found that states existing frameworks are technology-neutral and applicable to AI. The Knot speech of July 2024 is earlier but speaks of frameworks to assess risks and is recorded as context. The IMF Departmental Paper of 2021 relays that some jurisdictions concluded existing rules suffice, but its authors contest the proposition. Contesting texts: IMF Departmental Paper 2021; ESRB ASC Report No 16 (2025).
- **U01** (us-asia). Fed speeches index via web search (federalreserve.gov, 'AI' speeches 2018-2026); Brainard 2018, Barr Feb/May/Nov 2025, Bowman 2024, Cook 2023/2024/2026, Waller 2025/2026, Jefferson 2025 read; Fed FSR Nov 2025 grepped for 'herd', 'correlated'; BoJ speech search (boj.or.jp: AI, generative AI) — Kuroda 2017, Ueda 2024/2026, FSR annexes Oct 2024 and Aug 2026 read; PBoC plans 2019 and 2022-2025 grepped for 羊群/人工智能; RBI FREE-AI report and Das 2024 grepped; MAS AIRM consultation, SAFR paper read. Run 2026-11-02. _Earliest:_ Kuroda (13 April 2017) is the earliest text found that links AI applied to trading with volatility from similar algorithms. Barr (Feb 2025) uses 'herding' explicitly; the PBoC plan of December 2021 names 羊群效应 without the mechanism (bears_on). The Fed FSR of November 2025 contests the premise.
- **U02** (us-asia). Grep for 'concentration', 'third-party', 'few providers' across all retrieved Fed, PBoC, BoJ, MAS, HKMA and RBI texts; Brainard 2018 (vendor reliance), Bowman 2024 (external parties), Fed FSR Nov 2025 (common AI models, relayed), PBoC 2022-2025 plan item (二十四) (第三方算力设施集中度风险), MAS consultation 2025, RBI FREE-AI report pp. 20 and 73, Das 2024. _Earliest:_ Das (14 October 2024) is the earliest text found that states provider concentration and system-wide cascade together. The PBoC plan of December 2021 names concentration risk of third-party computing capacity and cloud services transmitting into finance, but not tied to AI explicitly, so it was not recorded (see omissions). Brainard 2018 notes reliance on nonbank AI vendors without a concentration claim.
- **U03** (us-asia). Grep for 'fraud', 'phishing', 'deepfake', 'cyber' in all retrieved texts; MAS circulars (GenAI cyber risks July 2024) located via mas.gov.sg search; BoJ FSR annex Oct 2024 (prompt injection); Das 2024; Waller 2026 (cyberattacks). _Earliest:_ Brainard (13 November 2018) on AI-personalized phishing is the earliest found. MAS (July 2024) is the institution-voice text recorded. BoJ annex (Oct 2024), Das (Oct 2024) and Waller (Sept 2026) also meet the criterion and were not recorded to keep the set bounded.
- **U04** (us-asia). Grep for 'black box', 'explainab', 'opaque', 算法黑盒 across retrieved texts; interagency RFI 2021 read; HKMA 2019 principles; MAS AI MRM information paper Dec 2024; BoJ FSR annex Oct 2024; Das 2024. _Earliest:_ Brainard (13 November 2018) is the earliest found; the interagency RFI (31 March 2021) is the earliest in the institution voice. HKMA's principles (1 November 2019) require explainability ('no black-box excuse') without stating that opacity hampers risk management; not recorded.
- **U05** (us-asia). Grep for 'nowcast', 'forecast', 'monetary policy' with 'AI' in Fed speeches, BoJ speeches, PBoC Pan 2026; Brainard 2018 footnote 8; Cook 2023; Bowman 2024; Ueda 2026; Pan 2026. FEDS Notes not searched systematically. _Earliest:_ Bowman (22 November 2024) is the earliest articulation found. Brainard 2018 (footnote 8) says AI tools are likely useful for monetary policy without naming analysis (not recorded for U05). Cook (22 September 2023) conditions the value of AI forecasts on explanation (recorded as contesting). Pan (October 2026) states the PBoC will explore AI for monetary-policy situation analysis (not recorded to keep the set bounded).
- **U06** (us-asia). Fed speeches on AI and productivity/labour/monetary policy 2023-2026 (web search on federalreserve.gov; Cook 2023, 2024, 2026; Barr May 2025, Feb 2026; Bowman 2024; Waller Oct 2025; Jefferson Nov 2025); PBoC: Chen Yulu 2018/2019 speeches (no AI), Pan Gongsheng 2026 Financial Street Forum; BoJ Ueda 2024/2026; RBI Das 2024. Powell press-conference remarks not searched. _Earliest:_ Cook (22 September 2023) is the earliest found that ties AI's effects to the dual mandate and monetary policy. Bowman (22 November 2024) mentions the natural rates of unemployment and interest in one sentence. Barr (9 May 2025) is the most developed (u*, r*). No contesting text was found; Barr (17 February 2026) and Cook (24 February 2026) state that monetary policy is not suited to structural change, which qualifies but does not dispute the criterion.
- **U07** (us-asia). Web search federalreserve.gov for 'AI agents payments', 'agentic'; Waller 20 Aug 2025, 29 Sep 2025, 21 Oct 2025, 24 Feb 2026, 29 Sep 2026; Fed roundtable summary 26 May 2026; Barr Feb 2025; MAS SAFR white paper and release (July 2026), Chia Der Jiun speech 13 Nov 2025, MAS parliamentary reply 5 Aug 2026; HKMA press releases (Project SAGE, 2 Nov 2026). _Earliest:_ Waller (29 September 2025) is the earliest found that states AI agents executing tasks for persons or firms as a payments development. Barr (18 February 2025) says new institutions might be needed to facilitate transactions among households, businesses and GenAI agents, without naming payments (not recorded); Waller (20 August 2025) calls agentic AI the next wave without describing agents paying.
- **U08** (us-asia). Grep for 'stablecoin', 'tokeni', 'programmable' with 'agent' or 'AI' in Waller 2025/2026 speeches, Ueda 2026, Chia 2025, MAS SAFR paper, HKMA releases, PBoC Pan 2026; Fed roundtable summary 26 May 2026 (no substantive text). _Earliest:_ No text in the institution or official voice was found that meets the criterion. Waller's speeches discuss stablecoins and AI agents separately; Ueda (3 March 2026) links AI agents and blockchain-based services (bears_on). The MAS SAFR industry white paper (July 2026) mentions stablecoin and Purpose Bound Money controls for agent-initiated transactions but is industry-developed and not used (see omissions).
- **U09** (us-asia). Grep for 'suptech', 'supervis', 监管科技, 'surveillance' across retrieved texts; PBoC 2019 and 2022-2025 plans; Fed FSR Nov 2025; Barr Nov 2025; Pan 2026; HKMA AML circular 2024. _Earliest:_ Brainard (13 November 2018, footnote 8) is the earliest found. The PBoC 2019-2021 plan describes supervisory technology without naming AI or machine learning; the 2022-2025 plan (December 2021) names natural language processing and pattern recognition.
- **U10** (us-asia). Grep for 'fraud detection', 'credit', 'anti-money', 风控 in retrieved texts; Barr Nov 2025's citation of Phillips 1997 followed to the Board's 1997 speech archive; PBoC 2019 plan; HKMA 2024 AML circular; Bowman 2024; Waller Aug 2025; Kuroda 2017. _Earliest:_ Phillips (4 November 1997) is the earliest found, located through a later citation. Kuroda (2017) lists risk management among AI uses without stating improvement; Brainard (2018), the PBoC plan (August 2019), Bowman (2024), Waller (August 2025) and HKMA (September 2024) restate the claim.
- **U11** (us-asia). Fed compliance plans under OMB M-24-10 (Sept 2024) and M-25-21 (Sept 2025); Fed AI programme page; Bowman 2024; Barr Feb and Nov 2025; Waller Feb 2026; Cook Feb 2026; Ueda 2024 and 2026; PBoC Pan 2026; BoJ Kouguchi Jan 2025 (located, not retrieved). _Earliest:_ Bowman (22 November 2024) is the earliest found that states a need to build staff expertise and capacity. The Board's compliance plan (September 2024) describes its AI programme and talent development without arguing a need (recorded as an event). Barr (11 November 2025) is the most developed.
- **U12** (us-asia). Grep for 'existing', 'technology-neutral', 'framework' with 'AI' in retrieved texts; Brainard 2018; Bowman 2024; interagency RFI 2021; PBoC 2019 and 2022-2025 plans; MAS FEAT 2018, MindForge whitepaper, AIRM consultation 2025; HKMA 2019 principles; RBI FREE-AI report; Ueda 2024; Waller Oct 2025. _Earliest:_ Brainard (13 November 2018) is the earliest articulation found. The PBoC plan (August 2019) is the earliest contesting text found (contrary-only); the RBI FREE-AI report (August 2025) engages the premise. Ueda (21 November 2024) says current financial regulations may not be fully equipped for new types of financial services from technology generally, not AI specifically (not recorded).

## Leads not recorded

Texts located in searches but outside the bodies the scope names, or not read, by maintainer decisions 10a and 16a. They are not records and hold no role; the collection’s "How we searched" note names them.

- **U09.** Scott W. Bauguess (Deputy Director and Deputy Chief Economist, Division of Economic and Risk Analysis, U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission), “Has Big Data Made Us Lazy?”, speech of 21 October 2016, and his speech of 21 June 2017 (both with the speaker’s own-views note): machine learning used by SEC staff to assess market risk and to direct examination and investigative staff. Read by the cross-family checker on sec.gov (A1.9: https://www.sec.gov/news/speech/bauguess-american-accounting-association-102116.html and https://www.sec.gov/newsroom/speeches-statements/bauguess-big-data-ai). The 2017 speech mentions a 2015 talk, not read. — The SEC is a securities regulator outside the named bodies (decision 10a). Both speeches precede the FSB report of 27 June 2017, U09's earliest located institutional text; U09's earliest located text is now BCBS Working Paper No. 4 (December 2000). (follow-up)
- **U05.** Hlaváček, Koňák and Čada, Czech National Bank Working Paper 11/2005 (December 2005, staff): the official abstract reports that a neural-network model forecasts currency in circulation better, for liquidity forecasting. Located by the cross-family checker (A1.5); the paper itself was not read. Abstract: https://www.cnb.cz/en/economic-research/research-publications/cnb-working-paper-series/The-Application-of-Structured-Feedforward-Neural-Networks-to-the-Modelling-of-Daily-Series-of-Currency-in-Circulation-00001 — The Czech National Bank is outside the named bodies (decision 16a). The text is later than U05's earliest located text (ECB Working Paper No. 352, April 2004) and earlier than its earliest located institutional text (FSB, November 2017). (follow-up)
- **U05.** Bank of Canada Working Paper 99-3, “Forecasting GDP Growth Using Artificial Neural Networks”, by Greg Tkacz and Sarah Hu (January 1999, staff, with an authors’ views disclaimer), reported by the cross-family checker after its blind search (A1.5, first check) and read by it in the second check (C33) from the Bank of Canada repository PDF: neural networks improve four-quarter GDP growth forecasts significantly, one-quarter forecasts not significantly. Not read in this environment (empty HTTP 202 responses). — The Bank of Canada is outside the named bodies (decision 16a); a lead, with no role. Agreed by the checker (C33). (follow-up)
- **U04, U12 and others.** BaFin, “Big data meets artificial intelligence – challenges and implications for the supervision and regulation of financial services”, report of 16 July 2018 put to public consultation (https://www.bafin.de/SharedDocs/Downloads/EN/dl_bdai_studie_en.pdf; read 2026-11-04 by text search only). It discusses black-box models (PDF pp. 14, 37) and says that supervisory and regulatory bodies “must be technology-neutral” (p. 4). Cited by the 2021 BaFin–Bundesbank paper. — BaFin alone is not among the bodies the scope names; the scope's opening names “banking and market supervisors” in general. Whether a national supervisor that is not a central bank enters is a scope question (open question in the review note). It would not be earlier than U04’s holder (June 2017); on U12 it would precede Brainard (November 2018) only if a passage states broad coverage, which the text search did not find. Maintainer decision of 2026-11-04 (open question 1 of the follow-up pass): as recommended, the BDAI report stays a lead, on the logic of decision 16a. (follow-up)
- **U04, U12.** Deutsche Bundesbank, discussion paper “The Use of Artificial Intelligence and Machine Learning in the Financial Sector”, November 2020, cited by the 2021 BaFin–Bundesbank paper (PDF p. 3). — Within the scope (Bundesbank) but not read in this bounded pass; later than the U04 and U12 holders, and later than DNB 2019 as U12’s institutional comparator. (follow-up)
- **U05.** Bank of England, “One Bank Research Agenda”, discussion paper, February 2015 (launched 25 February 2015), read by the cross-family checker in a capture of 19 March 2015 of https://www.bankofengland.co.uk/research/Documents/onebank/discussion.pdf (web.archive.org; SHA-256 a1963d08…15e2), Theme 4, printed pp. 23–24: “textual information has been quantified systematically using machine-learning algorithms”. — Agreed with the checker (second check, C32): it meets U05 in institution voice and would be U05’s earliest located institutional text. Not yet recorded: web.archive.org could not be reached from this environment on 2026-11-04, so the passage is not checked against a cached copy. To be drafted before admission part 2 (U05–U08). The checker’s caution stands: the adjacent Google-query nowcasting example is not stated to use machine learning. (follow-up)

- Christine Lagarde, “The transformative power of AI” (ECB, 1 April 2025) — Read; on productivity, investment and labour, with no passage tying AI’s effects to monetary policy, so it does not meet U06. (europe)

(europe)

- Bank of Finland blog “Tekoäly tukee keskuspankkien analyysityötä” (Joni Heikkinen, Euro & talous, 2025) — Retrieved; on text analysis of ECB communication with AI models (U05). No publication date could be read from the retrieved page, so it was not recorded. (europe)
- ECB FSR May 2024 sentence on the current regulatory framework (U12) — States that targeted initiatives may be needed if concerns arise that the current framework cannot tackle; too conditional to record without stretching U12. (europe)
- Bundesbank/BaFin consultation paper “Machine learning in risk models” (July 2021) and its results paper (February 2022) — Located by discovery search; Wuermeling’s speech reports the consultation results and is recorded instead. The papers were not retrieved for lack of time. (europe)
- Sveriges Riksbank Annual Report 2024 (strategic partnership with AI Sweden) and Thedéen’s hearing of 2025 (data analytics and AI in the strategic plan 2024–27) — Located by discovery search only; not retrieved. Candidate U11 texts and events for the integrator. (europe)

It states the meeting’s topic, not that AI agents make payments (U07 not met), and prints no publication date (the server’s last-modified date is 3 September 2026), so its wording cannot be placed against U07’s term record (Breeden, 30 June 2026). Not recorded. The cross-family checker could not settle its posting date either (second check, C31): 26 May 2026 is the meeting date; the note is linked from Waller’s speech dated 29 September 2026 (footnote 7), which dates the link, not the note. Publication stays unknown; the filename and the server’s last-modified date are not used. (follow-up)

Not the authority's own text; the MindForge paper's statement that existing TRM, cloud and outsourcing guidelines largely cover GenAI (U12) and SAFR's stablecoin/PBM passage (U08) are therefore not recorded. Ueda 2024's statement on regulation concerns technology generally, not AI. (us-asia)

- FEDS Notes and other Fed staff papers (e.g. FEDS 2025-090, 2025-092) — Not searched systematically; staff voice selectively per the task, and none was needed for an earliest-located role in this group. (us-asia)

## Retrieval failures

- https://www.bis.org/speeches/sp251009.htm: HTTP 403 after redirect to https://www.bis.org/speeches/20251008-artificial-intelligence-and-central-banks-monetary-and-financial-stability-implications-0. none; speaker not identified (discovery snippet mentions AI-driven volatility and herding) (bis-basel)
- https://www.bis.org/speeches/sp240417.pdf: Redirected to the HTML speech page, which carries only the opening paragraphs; the full-text PDF was not reached. Opening paragraphs on https://www.bis.org/speeches/20240416-managing-ai-banking-are-we-ready-cooperate used for one bears_on record (bis-basel)
- https://www.bis.org/speeches/sp190604a.pdf: HTTP 404. HTML page of the column used (bis-basel)
- https://www.dnb.nl/media/jkbip2jc/general-principles-for-the-use-of-artificial-intelligence-in-the-financial-sector.pdf: HTTP 403 on 2026-11-02 (518-byte response). None. DNB’s “General principles for the use of Artificial Intelligence in the financial sector” (2019) is a candidate earlier text for U04 and U12; for Codex retrieval. (europe)
- https://acpr.banque-france.fr/: Not attempted: banque-france.fr refused this environment (HTTP 403) per the brief. None. ACPR’s discussion document on governance of AI in finance (2020) is a candidate for U04; for Codex retrieval. (europe)
- https://www.dnb.nl/media/jkbip2jc/general-principles-for-the-use-of-artificial-intelligence-in-the-financial-sector.pdf: HTTP 403 to curl on 2026-11-04. the same address through the session's web-fetch tool returned the PDF (SHA-256 recorded on the source); DNB's release of July 2019 not reached; both on the 2026-11-04 brief's Part C (follow-up)
- https://www.bankofengland.co.uk/-/media/boe/files/research/one-bank-research-agenda---discussion-paper.pdf: HTTP 404 (address guessed from the summary's). the summary and the launch speech were read (no AI or machine-learning passage); brief Part C (follow-up)
- Danmarks Nationalbank Analysis of 21 January 2019 (PDF): no PDF link found on the landing page. the landing page summary was read and recorded; brief Part C (follow-up)
- https://www.imf.org/-/media/Files/Publications/GFSR/2024/October/English/ch3.ashx: HTTP 403. IMF eLibrary HTML of the same chapter (elibrary.imf.org/display/book/9798400277573/CH003.xml), recorded. (international)
- https://www.elibrary.imf.org/downloadpdf/view/journals/087/2021/024/087.2021.issue-024-en.pdf: HTTP 202 with empty body. IMF eLibrary HTML full text (article-A001), recorded; the PDF may carry the series disclaimer and printed page numbers. (international)
- https://www.imf.org/en/news/articles/2024/06/06/sp060624-imagining-tomorrow-s-financial-stability-challenges: HTTP 403. None. IMF speech of 6 June 2024 on financial stability challenges, reported to discuss generative AI; speaker not confirmed. For Codex. (international)
- https://www.iosco.org/library/pubdocs/pdf/IOSCOPD684.pdf: HTTP 403. None. IOSCO final report FR06/2021, "The use of artificial intelligence and machine learning by market intermediaries and asset managers" (September 2021). For Codex. (international)
- https://www.iosco.org/library/pubdocs/pdf/IOSCOPD788.pdf: HTTP 403. None. IOSCO consultation report on artificial intelligence in capital markets (2025); address taken from IOSCO numbering, to be confirmed. For Codex. (international)
- http://www.pbc.gov.cn/goutongjiaoliu/113456/113469/3878634/index.html: HTTP 404 (also via https). The plan PDF from the PBoC government-information section was used; the news release announcing the 2019 plan was not retrieved. (us-asia)
- http://www.pbc.gov.cn/hanglingdao/128697/128722/128787/4113712/index.html: HTTP 404 (also via https): Fan Yifei, 2020 Financial Street Forum speech. None; old PBoC leadership-speech addresses appear to have moved. (us-asia)
- http://camlmac.pbc.gov.cn/hanglingdao/128697/128722/128787/4364939/index.html: HTTP 404: Fan Yifei, 2021 Financial Street Forum speech. None. (us-asia)

The PBoC reply of 27 October 2022 to CPPCC proposal 03114 was used for the standard's issuance; the number JR/T 0221-2021 and date 2021-03-26 rest on search-result snippets only and are not evidenced. (us-asia)

## Open questions from the drafts

- Should WP 1194 (staff, June 2024) or AER 2024 chapter III (institution, released 25 June 2024) head U01 for the BIS? The working paper's landing page date (13 June 2024) puts it earlier. _Recommended:_ Keep WP 1194 as earliest located (decided by date of wording across voices) and show AER 2024 as the earliest institution-voice text. (bis-basel)
- Is the IFC Bulletin 44 overview (a relay of a Central Bank of Aruba presentation) acceptable as the earliest located U05 text, or should the Aruba paper in the same bulletin be recorded in its own right? _Recommended:_ Retrieve and record the Aruba paper (Central Bank of Aruba staff, same bulletin) in the integration pass; keep the overview as relay. (bis-basel)
- The full text of Hernández de Cos's April 2024 speech may contain a direct U01/U02 articulation earlier than WP 1194 and BCBS May 2024. _Recommended:_ Retrieve the PDF through Codex and re-check U01 and U02. (bis-basel)
- Should Project page 'Last updated' dates be used as the date of the text for events? _Recommended:_ Yes, as posting dates, with the event's own year in eventDate where the page gives it. (bis-basel)
- The ECB innovation platform call (ecb-2026-ip-call-ai-agents) prints no posting date; the record uses the year 2026 from the application deadline. _Recommended:_ Codex or the integrator: confirm the posting date from the ECB press and news listing; the address contains “260928”. (europe)
- U07: the earliest located text in this group is a relay (ECB outcome report, September 2025) of workshop participants’ ideas about AI wallets; the earliest in an official’s own voice is Breeden (30 June 2026). _Recommended:_ Integrator: decide whether a relay may hold earliest-located for U07 across groups, and compare with BIS and Federal Reserve texts on agentic payments. (europe)
- The ECB FSR special feature (May 2024) is recorded in staff voice (prepared by named authors, published as part of the Financial Stability Review, without a views disclaimer). _Recommended:_ Integrator: confirm voice treatment for FSR special features consistently across groups. (europe)
- U06: Carney (2018) frames AI within a “Fourth Industrial Revolution”. _Recommended:_ Audit: check whether this meets U06 as worded or should be context; Cipollone (2024) would then hold earliest-located in this group. (europe)
- BaFin’s own texts (the BDAI report of July 2018 and later BaFin principles): enter as a banking and market supervisor, or stay a lead as decision 16a keeps central banks outside the named list? _Recommended:_ Keep as a lead under the logic of 16a; the joint BaFin–Bundesbank paper of 2021 is recorded because the Bundesbank is a named body. Decided by the maintainer on 2026-11-04: as recommended. (follow-up)
- Voice of IMF GFSR chapter 3: the chapter carries a staff disclaimer but no named authors in the eLibrary expression. Recorded as institution voice with the disclaimer and statements naming "IMF staff of the Monetary and Capital Markets Department". _Recommended:_ Keep as institution voice with the staff disclaimer shown, or check the PDF for an author note (Codex) and switch to staff voice if authors are printed. (international)
- U12 earliest located: the FSB Chair’s speech of 11 July 2024 ("We already have frameworks …") predates GFSR chapter 3 but is recorded as context because it speaks of frameworks to assess risks. _Recommended:_ Integrator to confirm the conservative reading; if other groups have earlier technology-neutral statements (for example BCBS, HKMA or Bank of England texts), those take precedence. (international)
- No text contesting U01 (for example, a statement that AI-driven herding has not been observed) was found as a separate stance; the FSB 2017 texts state that there was no evidence to date, and the FSB 2024 report that LLM use for internal efficiencies would have minimal effect. These are recorded as context evidence on the articulating records. _Recommended:_ Leave as context unless another group finds a text that disputes the proposition itself. (international)
- ESRB ASC Report No 16 states both that existing policy tools are likely sufficient with recalibration and that some risks may require fundamental changes beyond the current prudential framework. Recorded as contesting U12 with the "sufficient" passage as context. _Recommended:_ Audit should check this reading. (international)
- The FSB letter of 28 August 2026 is dated by signature (28 August) and published on 31 August 2026. _Recommended:_ Keep the signature date. (international)
- Which voice applies to a committee report placed on a central bank's website (RBI FREE-AI Committee, with external members and an RBI member secretary)? _Recommended:_ Keep 'staff' for now with the committee named as authors, or add a 'committee' voice at integration; in either case never attribute it to the RBI as policy. (us-asia)
- Should MAS-convened industry white papers (MindForge 2024, SAFR 2026) be admitted as evidence in an 'institution' or other voice? _Recommended:_ Treat as industry texts outside scope (as MAS itself labels them), and rely on MAS media releases and guidelines for MAS's own statements. (us-asia)
- Is Phillips (1997) enough to hold the earliest-located role for U10, given that AI is one item in a list? _Recommended:_ Keep it as earliest located with the qualification; the integrator may prefer a fuller statement (Brainard 2018 or the PBoC 2019 plan) as 'most developed'. (us-asia)
- Should the PBoC standard JR/T 0221-2021 be dated 2021-03-26? _Recommended:_ Retrieve the standard's record or text from cfstc.pbc.gov.cn with a script-capable browser before giving an event date. (us-asia)
